25 September 2026 — The counting principle: per-order lower bounds follow from constructions

Working proof: the counting principle, for every additive grid in characteristic 2. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The root-curve lemma proved the four-element square theorem in dimension two, using the exact kernel of \(Z\mapsto z\). Does the method reach every dimension, and does it reprove the laws already known?

Setting. \(F\) has characteristic 2, and \(S\subseteq F\) is an \(\mathbb F_2\)-subspace of size \(s=2^\sigma\ge2\), with \(L=\prod_{t\in S}(x-t)\) and additive root \(x(y)\). Let \(n\ge1\), \(K=F(y_1,\ldots,y_n)\), and \(z_i=x(y_iT)\in K[[T]]\), so \(L(z_i)=y_iT\). \(\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)\) is the set of \(T\)-orders of nonzero \(K\)-combinations of the monomials \(z^e\) with \(|e|\le d\). Savings are \(h=n(s-1)+s(k-1)-\delta_S(n,k,\ell)\), and \(m=k-\ell-1\). Put

\[ N_{s,n}(d)=\#\{(Q,r)\in\mathbb N\times[0,s-1]^n:\ sQ+|r|\le d\}, \]

the partial sums of the coefficients of \((1+t+\cdots+t^{s-1})^n/(1-t^s)\).

The root-curve lemma in \(n\) variables

Working lemma (lem:root-curve-independence).

  1. If some \(P\) with multiplicity \(\ge k\) at the nonzero points of \(S^n\) and order exactly \(\ell\) at the origin has savings \(\ge h\), then \(m\notin\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(h-1)\).
  2. The elements \(T^Qz^r\), \(Q\ge0\), \(r\in[0,s-1]^n\), are \(K\)-linearly independent. Hence \(|\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)|\ge N_{s,n}(d)\).

Proof of (a). This is the proof of the two-variable lemma (a), word for word, with \(n\) variables.

  • Degree accounting. The term \(x^\varepsilon y^e\) with \(\varepsilon_i=s-1-a_i\) and \(|e|=k-1-t\) has degree \(n(s-1)+s(k-1)-(|a|+st)\).
  • Triangular replacement. The coefficient formula is stated for \(n\) variables and \(s\ge4\). Its proof and facts (a)–(c) hold verbatim for \(s=2\), where \(\Lambda_1=c_0^{-1}\) and \(\Lambda_0=c_0^{-1}(x+c_0)\); only the later remark on \(\Lambda_{s-2}\) needs \(s\ge4\). The replacement of \(\Lambda_{s-1-a_i}\) by \(x^{a_i}\) works factor by factor, since the leading coefficient \(\binom{s-1}{a_i}\) is odd. The lower terms of \(\prod_i\Lambda_{s-1-a_i}(x_i)\) are \(x^{a'}\) with \(a'\le a\) componentwise and \(|a'|<|a|\), so their conditions have the same \(t\) and smaller cost, and induction on \(|a|\) applies.
  • The functional. \(\Lambda(G)=\sum_{c\le m}w^{(c)}[T^c]G\) kills \(T^tz^a\) for \(a_i\le s-1\) and \(|a|+st\le h-1\). This holds trivially for \(t>m\).
  • All monomials. The reduction \(z_i^s=y_iT+\sum_{j<\sigma}c_jz_i^{2^j}\) extends this to all \(z^e\) with \(|e|\le h-1\).
  • Conclusion. An element of order \(m\) in their span would give \(0=\gamma w^{(m)}\ne0\).

Proof of (b). Let \(E=K(T)\) and \(E_j=E(z_1,\ldots,z_j)\). We show \([E_j:E_{j-1}]=s\) for each \(j\). Then \(\{z^r:r\in[0,s-1]^n\}\) is a basis of \(E_n/E\). A \(K\)-linear relation among the \(T^Qz^r\) is an \(E\)-linear relation among the \(z^r\), so it is trivial.

  • Conjugates form a coset. The roots of \(L(Z)-y_jT\) are \(z_j+t\), \(t\in S\subseteq F\). Let \(p\) be the minimal polynomial of \(z_j\) over \(E_{j-1}\). If \(z_j+t\) is a root of \(p\), then \(p(Z+t)\) is monic, irreducible, of the same degree, and vanishes at \(z_j\), so \(p(Z+t)=p(Z)\). Hence the roots of \(p\) are \(z_j+S'\) for a subgroup \(S'\le S\), and \(p(Z)=L_{S'}(Z-z_j)=L_{S'}(Z)-L_{S'}(z_j)\), where \(L_{S'}=\prod_{t\in S'}(Z-t)\) is additive.
  • A proper subgroup gives a rational function. Suppose \(S'\ne S\). Then \(\beta=L_{S'}(z_j)\in E_{j-1}\). Also \(L=L_2\circ L_{S'}\), where \(L_2\) is the subspace polynomial of \(L_{S'}(S)\), of degree \(s/|S'|\ge2\). So \(L_2(\beta)=L(z_j)=y_jT\).
  • \(y_j\) is transcendental over the rest. Let \(E'\) be the subfield generated over \(F\) by \(y_i\) (\(i\ne j\)), \(T\) and \(z_1,\ldots,z_{j-1}\). Then \(E_{j-1}=E'(y_j)\), and \(y_j\) is transcendental over \(E'\). Indeed \(E'\) lies in \(k((T))\) with \(k=F(y_i:i\ne j)\), and \(y_j\) is transcendental over \(k((T))\). A relation \(\sum c_i(T)y_j^i=0\), scaled to have coefficients in \(k[[T]]\) not all divisible by \(T\), reduces modulo \(T\) to a nontrivial relation over \(k\).
  • Degree in \(y_j\). So \(\beta\) is a rational function of \(y_j\) over \(E'\), of some degree \(\rho\). Then \(L_2(\beta)\) has degree \(\rho\deg L_2\), which is \(0\) or at least 2. But \(y_jT\) has degree 1. Hence \(S'=S\), and \([E_j:E_{j-1}]=s\).
  • The count. \(T=L(z_1)/y_1\) has degree \(s\) in \(z_1\). So \(T^Qz^r\) lies in the span of the monomials of degree \(\le sQ+|r|\), and those with \(sQ+|r|\le d\) are \(N_{s,n}(d)\) independent elements of the span. A subspace of \(K[[T]]\) has as many orders as its dimension. \(\square\)

The counting principle

Working theorem (thm:root-curve-counting-principle). Let \(H:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N\) satisfy two conditions.

  1. Attained. For every \(m\ge0\) there are \((k,\ell)\) with \(k-\ell-1=m\) and a polynomial with multiplicity \(\ge k\) at the nonzero points of \(S^n\), order exactly \(\ell\) at the origin, and degree \(\le n(s-1)+s(k-1)-H(m)\). One \(\ell\) per \(m\) suffices.
  2. Counted. \(\#\{m:H(m)\le d\}=N_{s,n}(d)\) for every \(d\ge0\).

Then every polynomial with multiplicity \(\ge k\) at the nonzero points of \(S^n\) and order exactly \(\ell\) at the origin has savings \(\le H(k-\ell-1)\), for all \(0\le\ell<k\). So \(\delta_S(n,k,\ell)\ge n(s-1)+s(k-1)-H(m)\) everywhere, with equality wherever the construction of (1) applies.

Proof.

  • Inclusion. Let \(m\in\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)\). By (1) and lemma (a), \(m\notin\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(H(m)-1)\). Since \(\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}\) grows with \(d\), this forces \(d\ge H(m)\). So \(\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)\subseteq\{m:H(m)\le d\}\).
  • Equality. By lemma (b) and (2), \(|\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)|\ge N_{s,n}(d)=\#\{m:H(m)\le d\}\). So the two sets are equal.
  • Lower bound. If some \(P\) has savings \(h\) at \(m\), lemma (a) gives \(m\notin\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(h-1)=\{H\le h-1\}\), so \(h\le H(m)\). \(\square\)

The inclusion step and lemma (b) show more: every law \(H\) satisfying (1) has \(\#\{m:H(m)\le d\}\ge N_{s,n}(d)\) for all \(d\). Condition (2) says that \(H\) meets this bound with equality, and then it is optimal.

Three lower bounds by counting

Working corollary (cor:root-curve-lower-bounds). The counting principle gives new proofs of the lower bounds in these three results.

  • Binary. The all-dimension binary theorem: \(s=2\), \(S=\{0,1\}\), \(H(m)=2Q+s_2(r)\) for \(m=Q2^n+r\).
  • Base \(q\). The \(q\)-ary theorem for \(q=2^\sigma\): \(S=\mathbb F_q\), \(H(m)=qQ+s_q(r)\) for \(m=Qq^n+r\).
  • Four elements. The four-element square theorem, which this reproves with the exact kernel replaced by lemma (b): \(S\) a four-element subspace with \(c_1\ne0\), \(n=2\), \(H(m)=2\lfloor m/8\rfloor+\lceil(m\bmod8)/2\rceil\). On a scaled \(\mathbb F_4\) the base-4 law applies instead; it has the same count but different values.

Proof. In each case condition (1) is the recorded upper bound.

  • Binary. The construction \(y_1^\ell F^{2q}g_{r+1}\), for every \(\ell\).
  • Base \(q\). The construction \((x_1^q-x_1)^\ell F^{qQ}P(1)\) of the \(q\)-ary theorem.
  • Four elements. The upper half.

Condition (2) is a bijection or a generating-function identity.

  • Base \(q\), including \(q=2\). Write \(r=\sum_{j<n}r_jq^j\) in base \(q\). Then \(m\mapsto(Q,(r_0,\ldots,r_{n-1}))\) is a bijection \(\mathbb N\to\mathbb N\times[0,q-1]^n\) with \(H(m)=qQ+|r|\). So \(\#\{H\le d\}=N_{q,n}(d)\).
  • Four elements. \(N_{4,2}\) is the partial-sum sequence of \((1+t+t^2+t^3)^2/(1-t^4)=(1+t+t^2+t^3)/(1-t)\), whose coefficients, the level counts, are \(1,2,3,4,4,4,\ldots\). The values of \(H\) have the same level counts: 1, 2 and 3 values of \(m\) at levels 0, 1 and 2, and four at every level \(\ge3\), as counted in the theorem's proof. \(\square\)

For the binary theorem this replaces steps 2–5 of the lower bound: the multi-additive forms, their vanishing on the span of the \(y_i\), and the reduction modulo the subspace polynomial. What remains is the coefficient formula, a functional, a field-degree count and the construction. The four-element proof no longer needs the kernel of \(Z\mapsto z\).

Other characteristics. Nothing above uses \(p=2\) beyond notation. \(\binom{s-1}{a}\not\equiv0\) modulo \(p\) for \(s=p^\sigma\) by Lucas; the Lagrange constant is \(\prod_{t\ne0}(-t)\); and the degree argument in (b) is unchanged. So the principle should hold for \(\mathbb F_p\)-subspaces in characteristic \(p\), and reprove the \(q\)-ary lower bound for every prime power. For general subspaces the coefficient formula is recorded only for characteristic 2; for \(S=\mathbb F_q\) it is recorded in every characteristic (\(q\)-ary formula). What remains for odd \(q\) is to write lemma (a) and (b) in characteristic \(p\), so this is stated as a remark, not a claim.

Checks. research/tools/bmd_root_curve_orders_n.py computes \(\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)\) at random \(y\in\mathbb F_{2^{16}}\) and compares it with \(\{m:H(m)\le d\}\).

  • Binary, \(n=3\), \(d\le7\): the sets are equal, with sizes \(1,4,8,\ldots,28\).
  • Binary, \(n=4\), \(d\le5\): equal.
  • \(S=\mathbb F_4\), \(n=2\), \(d\le6\): equal.

Each output file holds one random trial; outputs are in research/results/bmd-r18-root-curve-reach/.

General statement. thm:root-curve-counting-principle, for every \(\mathbb F_2\)-subspace \(S\) of a field of characteristic 2, all \(n\ge1\) and all \(0\le\ell<k\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the coefficient formula and its facts (whose recorded setting is \(s\ge4\); the \(s=2\) case is addressed in the proof of (a), which also closes the same gap in the two-variable lemma), the two-variable root-curve lemma, and the upper-bound constructions of the three laws. Claim searches (root curve, linear disjointness, additive extensions, Hilbert function) found no prior version. A fresh-context referee checked lemma (a) in \(n\) variables, the linear-disjointness proof in (b) step by step, the logic of the principle (no circularity; condition (1) is needed for every \(m\)), the digit bijection, and, by its own script, \(\#\{H\le d\}=N_{s,n}(d)\) for \(d\le30\) (binary \(n\le4\), \(q=4\) with \(n\le3\), four elements) and the agreement of the recorded formulas with these \(H\) for \(k<40\). Its required changes are made: the \(s=2\) coefficient formula, the hypothesis \(c_1\ne0\) in the four-element corollary, the precise form of condition (1), the monotonicity line in (a), the notation \(\sigma=\log_2s\), the level counts, the check sizes, the status line, the hedge on other characteristics, and the claim about \(\kappa(m)\), which is now an open question.

Assessment. The per-order problem on additive grids now has a two-part structure. Lower bounds are free once a construction meets the count \(N_{s,n}\). The only hard part is the construction, which depends on the arithmetic of \(S\). For the preprint, the binary lower bound gets a proof of about a page, which is the user's decision to use. For the research line, the natural next target is the sharpness question: is \(|\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)|=N_{s,n}(d)\) always? For \(n=2\) it is: the exact kernel of the two-variable lemma gives \(\binom{d+2}{2}-\binom{d+2-s}{2}\), which is \(N_{s,2}(d)\), since \((1+\cdots+t^{s-1})^2/(1-t^s)=(1-t^s)/(1-t)^2\). Sharpness alone would not give the large-\(\ell\) savings: lemma (a) bounds the savings by \(\kappa(m)=\min\{d:m\in\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)\}\), and equality needs a converse of (a), a jet of order \(\ell\) realizing any admissible functional once \(\ell\) is large. That converse is a separate open question; with it, the problem on every additive grid would reduce to computing orders on the root curve.

25 September 2026 — The stable per-order law on every additive grid: orders on the root curve

Working proof: for large ℓ the savings on every additive grid are the root-curve orders κ(m). Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The counting principle left two questions open. Is the count \(|\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)|\ge N_{s,n}(d)\) sharp? And does lemma (a) have a converse, so that the savings reach \(\kappa(m)=\min\{d:m\in\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)\}\) once \(\ell\) is large? Both hold. So on every additive grid the per-order problem has a stable law, read off the root curve, with the universal count \(N_{s,n}\).

Setting. As in the previous entry: \(S\) is an \(\mathbb F_2\)-subspace of size \(s=2^\sigma\ge2\) of a field \(F\) of characteristic 2, \(n\ge1\), \(z_i=x(y_iT)\), and \(K=F(y_1,\ldots,y_n)\). \(\Phi=n(s-1)+s(k-1)\), and the savings are \(h(k,\ell)=\Phi-\delta_S(n,k,\ell)\), with \(m=k-\ell-1\).

Sharpness of the count

Working lemma (lem:root-curve-sharpness). \(|\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)|=N_{s,n}(d)\) for all \(d\).

Proof. "\(\ge\)" is lemma (b) of the \(n\)-variable lemma. For "\(\le\)", note that \(|\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)|\) is the dimension of \(K[Z]_{\le d}\) modulo the kernel of \(Z\mapsto z\) in degrees \(\le d\). The kernel contains \(G_i=y_1L(Z_i)+y_iL(Z_1)\) for \(i\ge2\).

  • One reduction step. Write \(L(Z)=Z^s+\lambda(Z)\), with \(\deg\lambda\le s/2\). Let \(Z^e\) have \(|e|\le d\) and \(e_i\ge s\) for some \(i\ge2\). Then
    \[ Z^e=y_1^{-1}Z^{e-s\epsilon_i}\bigl(G_i+y_1\lambda(Z_i)+y_iZ_1^s+y_i\lambda(Z_1)\bigr). \]
    Here \(Z^{e-s\epsilon_i}G_i\) lies in the kernel in degree \(\le d\). The terms with \(\lambda\) have smaller total degree. The term \(Z^{e-s\epsilon_i+s\epsilon_1}\) has the same degree and a smaller \(\sum_{i\ge2}e_i\).
  • Induction. By induction on \((|e|,\sum_{i\ge2}e_i)\), every monomial of degree \(\le d\) is congruent to a combination of monomials of degree \(\le d\) with \(e_i<s\) for all \(i\ge2\). Those are \(Z_1^{sQ}Z^r\) with \(r\in[0,s-1]^n\) and \(sQ+|r|\le d\), and there are \(N_{s,n}(d)\) of them. \(\square\)

So in degrees \(\le d\), the \(T^Qz^r\) and the \(z_1^{sQ}z^r\) with \(sQ+|r|\le d\) are both bases of the image of \(K[Z]_{\le d}\), and the kernel in degree \(\le d\) is spanned by the \(Z^fG_i\) with \(|f|+s\le d\). For \(n=1\) there are no \(G_i\) and the kernel is 0.

The stable per-order law

Working theorem (thm:stable-per-order-law). For every \(S\), \(n\ge1\) and \(m\ge0\):

  1. \(h(k,\ell)\le\kappa(m)\) for every \(\ell\ge0\) (with \(k=\ell+m+1\)), and \(\kappa(m)\le sm\);
  2. \(h(k,\ell)\) does not decrease as \(\ell\) grows with \(m\) fixed;
  3. \(h(k,\ell)=\kappa(m)\) for all sufficiently large \(\ell\);
  4. \(\#\{m:\kappa(m)\le d\}=N_{s,n}(d)\) for every \(d\).

Hence the stable savings \(\lim_{\ell}h\) are \(\kappa\): the least degree of a polynomial in \(z_1,\ldots,z_n\), with coefficients in \(K=F(y)\), whose \(T\)-order is exactly \(m\). The theorem gives no bound on the threshold \(\ell_0\).

Proof.

  • (1). Lemma (a) gives \(m\notin\mathrm{Ord}(h-1)\), so \(\kappa(m)\ge h\). Also \(T^m=(L(z_1)/y_1)^m\) has order \(m\) and degree \(sm\), so \(\kappa(m)\le sm\).
  • (2). Multiplying by \(L(x_1)\) maps an admissible polynomial for \((k,\ell)\) to one for \((k+1,\ell+1)\): its multiplicity rises by 1 at every point of \(S^n\), and its lowest form gains the factor \(c_0x_1\). Its degree rises by \(s\), and so does \(\Phi\).
  • (4). \(\#\{\kappa\le d\}=|\mathrm{Ord}(d)|\), and the sharpness lemma gives the value.
  • (3), the condition system. Put \(h=\kappa(m)\). By the subspace-grid reduction, \(\delta_S=\min_{\operatorname{ord}v=\ell}\deg P(v)\), and \(\deg P(v)\) is the largest \(|\varepsilon|+s|e|\) over its nonzero terms. The term with \(\varepsilon_i=s-1-a_i\) and \(|e|=k-1-t\) has degree \(\Phi-(|a|+st)\). Let \(X_{a,r}\) be the degree-\(r\) part of \(\prod_ix(y_i)^{a_i}\), so that \([T^c](T^tz^a)=X_{a,c-t}\). The triangular replacement works in both directions. Since \(\binom{s-1}{a_i}\) is odd, \(c_0\Lambda_{s-1-a_i}(x)=x^{a_i}+\)lower powers, so each \(\Lambda\)-product condition is a combination of \(x^{a'}\)-conditions with \(a'\le a\) componentwise and the same \(t\), and conversely. The condition set is closed downward in \(a\). Equations with \(t>m\) are trivial, so the restriction \(t\le k-1\) is harmless. So a jet \(v\) of order exactly \(\ell\) has savings \(\ge h\) if and only if the homogeneous parts \(w^{(c)}\) (degree \(k-1-c\), \(c\le m\)) of \(w=v(x(y))\) satisfy
    \[ \sum_{c\le m}w^{(c)}X_{a,c-t}=0\qquad(a\in[0,s-1]^n,\ |a|+st\le h-1). \]
    Every family of homogeneous \(w^{(c)}\) of degrees \(\ell+m-c\) with \(w^{(m)}\ne0\) comes from a jet of order exactly \(\ell\), since \(v\mapsto v(x(y))\) is an order-preserving bijection on truncated series.
  • (3), a solution over \(K\). Since \(m\notin\mathrm{Ord}(h-1)\), the image of the span \(U\) of the \(z^e\) with \(|e|\le h-1\) in \(K[[T]]/(T^{m+1})\) does not contain \(T^m\). So there is a \(K\)-linear \(\Lambda\) on \(K[[T]]/(T^{m+1})\) that vanishes on it, with \(\Lambda(T^m)=1\). By the reduction \(z_i^s=y_iT+\cdots\) and \(T=L(z_1)/y_1\), \(U\) contains the \(T^tz^a\) with \(a\in[0,s-1]^n\) and \(|a|+st\le h-1\), since each is a polynomial of degree \(|a|+st\) in \(z\); only this inclusion is used. So \(W_c=\Lambda(T^c)\) solves the system over \(K\), with \(W_m=1\).
  • (3), polynomial homogeneous solutions. Clearing denominators gives a solution with \(W_c\in F[y]\) and \(W_m\ne0\). Each \(X_{a,r}\) is homogeneous of degree \(r\). Write \(W_c^{[j]}\) for the degree-\(j\) component of \(W_c\). For every \(D\), the degree-\((D-t)\) part of \(\sum_cW_cX_{a,c-t}\) is \(\sum_cW_c^{[D-c]}X_{a,c-t}\), so the \(W_c^{[D-c]}\) again form a solution. Choose \(D\) with \(W_m^{[D-m]}\ne0\), and put \(\ell_0=D-m\ge0\) and \(k_0=D+1\). This gives a jet of order \(\ell_0\) with savings \(\ge h\), and by (2) every \(\ell\ge\ell_0\) has savings \(\ge h\). With (1), the savings equal \(\kappa(m)\). \(\square\)

What this says.

  • Full grids and four-element squares. On \(\mathbb F_q^n\) with \(q=2^\sigma\), including \(\mathbb F_2\) and scaled subfields by scaling and base change, the \(q\)-ary theorem gives savings \(qm-(q-1)\sum_{j<n}\lfloor m/q^j\rfloor\), independent of \(\ell\). The four-element square theorem (\(n=2\), \(c_1\ne0\)) also gives savings independent of \(\ell\). There \(\kappa\) is that law, and no \(\ell\)-dependence occurs.
  • Other grids. On other grids \(\kappa\) can depend on the arithmetic of \(S\). The small-\(\ell\) values can lie below \(\kappa\), which is the \(\ell\)-dependence seen in the subspace-grid data. What \(S\) cannot change is the count: every additive grid of size \(s\) has exactly \(N_{s,n}(d)\) stable orders of cost \(\le d\).

Checks. An eight-element subspace \(S=\{0,\ldots,7\}\subset\mathbb F_{1024}\) (modulus \(x^{10}+x^3+1\)), \(n=2\):

  • bmd_root_curve_orders_n.py subspace gives \(|\mathrm{Ord}(d)|=N_{8,2}(d)\) for \(d\le6\) in three random trials. All three give the same \(\kappa\): \(0,1,1,2,2,3,3,4\) for \(m\le7\), then \(2,3,3,4,4,5,5,6\), then \(\kappa(16)=4\), and so on.
  • For every \(m\) with \(\kappa(m)\le6\), the kernel values for \(k\le34\) never exceed \(\kappa(m)\), and at the largest \(\ell\) they equal it. For example, at \(m=16\) the savings are 2 for \(\ell\le5\) and 4 for \(\ell\ge6\). Files: research/results/bmd-r19-sharpness/compare-gf1024.txt and kappa-gf1024-S0-7.txt.
  • Kernel bug found and fixed. bmd_jet_orders_gf.cpp stored row entries as 8-bit integers, which is wrong for fields beyond \(\mathbb F_{256}\). The first \(\mathbb F_{1024}\) run gave savings 2 at \(m=2\) for one \(k\), which is impossible since \(\kappa(2)=1\). Row entries are now 16-bit, with a guard \(a\le16\).
  • All earlier outputs of this kernel (bmd-r13-subspace-grids) have \(a\le7\), so every element fit in 8 bits, and they are unaffected. The regression value at \(\mathbb F_8\), \(k=5\), is unchanged.

General statement. thm:stable-per-order-law, for every \(\mathbb F_2\)-subspace of a field of characteristic 2, all \(n\ge1\) and \(m\ge0\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the \(n\)-variable lemma (a), (b), the counting principle, the condition system, and the subspace-grid reduction. Claim searches (root curve, stable, orders, Hilbert function) found no prior version. A fresh-context referee checked the reduction identity and its induction, the count, the existence of \(\Lambda\), parts (1), (2) and (4), and the check files, and found the mathematics correct. It asked for these changes, all made: an explicit converse step and the link to \(\delta\) in (3), the definition of \(X_{a,r}\), consistent notation for homogeneous components, the scope \(n=2\) for four-element grids, the degree-bounded form of the closing remarks, and a factual correction to the kernel-bug note. The note had also called savings 10 at \(m=31\) impossible, but the corrected run records that value, which only says \(\kappa(31)\ge10\).

Assessment. The route item's general claim is now answered in the form it can take. The stable per-order law on every additive grid is the order function of the root curve, with a universal count. The exact law, including small \(\ell\), is known on full grids and on four-element square grids (\(n=2\)). What remains open is arithmetic: computing \(\kappa\) for a given \(S\) (the eight-element grids show it depends on \(S\)), and the threshold \(\ell_0\) at which it is reached. Neither bears on the thread's goal. The next step should therefore be a route review, weighing this line against the preprint's needs.

25 September 2026 — Route review of the grid extensions: the root curve settles the structure

Route review; the stable per-order law passes a dimension-three test it was not built on. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

  • The line's general claim. Determine the per-order least degree \(\delta_S(n,k,\ell)\) on additive grids \(S^n\) beyond \(\mathbb F_2^n\).
  • What is established.
    • Exact laws. Every full grid \(\mathbb F_q^n\) (\(q\)-ary theorem), and every four-element square grid with \(c_1\ne0\) (four-element square theorem).
    • The base-\(s\) law fails whenever \(c_1\ne0\), in every dimension (second-coefficient theorem).
    • The structure. On every additive grid in characteristic 2:
      • the savings are bounded by the root-curve orders \(\kappa(m)\), and equal them for large \(\ell\);
      • exactly \(N_{s,n}(d)\) values of \(\kappa\) are \(\le d\) (stable per-order law);
      • any attained law with that count is exact for every \(\ell\) (counting principle). This reproves the binary per-order lower bound in about a page.
  • What the goal needs. Nothing: \(D(n,k)\) over \(\mathbb F_2\) is proved and formalized. The line's value for the thread is the shorter counting proof of the per-order lower bound, and the reach it shows: the method is a statement about orders on a curve, not about \(\mathbb F_2\).

Falsification attempt: dimension three

The stable law was checked in the previous entry only for \(n=2\). Here is a test in dimension three: the four-element subspace \(\{0,1,\alpha,1+\alpha\}\subset\mathbb F_8\), where the kernel data show a threshold in \(\ell\) at \(m=16\) (savings 1 for \(\ell\le3\), 2 for \(\ell\ge4\)).

  • The prediction needs \(\kappa\) for \(n=3\), which a random specialization of \(y\) inside \(\mathbb F_8\) cannot give, since every choice there is special. So \(\mathbb F_8\) is embedded in \(\mathbb F_{512}\) (modulus \(x^9+x^4+1\); \(\alpha\mapsto336\), a root of \(X^3+X+1\) in the order-7 subgroup). bmd_root_curve_orders_n.py subspace computes \(\mathrm{Ord}(d)\) for \(d\le4\) at two random points.
  • One point is special: it has fewer orders than \(N_{4,3}(d)\) from \(d=2\) on. The other has \(|\mathrm{Ord}(d)|=N_{4,3}(d)=1,4,10,20,33\), and gives \(\kappa(16)=2\), \(\kappa(24)=3\) and \(\kappa(32)=2\), among others.
  • bmd_kappa_compare.py, which now uses the first trial with no count deficit, compares these with the kernel values for \(k\le34\) at every \(m\) with \(\kappa(m)\le4\). No savings exceed \(\kappa(m)\), and the largest \(\ell\) attains \(\kappa(m)\) everywhere. In particular, at \(m=16\) the threshold \(\ell=4\) takes the savings from 1 up to \(\kappa=2\).
  • Files: research/results/bmd-r20-grid-route-review/. The same comparison for the \(\mathbb F_{1024}\) data of the previous entry is unchanged.

The test could have failed in two ways: savings above \(\kappa\), or a large-\(\ell\) value below it. Neither occurred.

Assessment and next step

The line is structurally complete in characteristic 2. What remains is of two kinds.

  • Arithmetic. Computing \(\kappa\) for a given \(S\), and bounding the threshold \(\ell_0\). Both are special-case work of diminishing value.
  • Extension. The characteristic-\(p\) version. The lemmas use characteristic 2 only in notation, and the coefficient formula is recorded in every characteristic for \(S=\mathbb F_q\). It would give a counting proof of the \(q\)-ary lower bound for every prime power, and the stable law for \(\mathbb F_p\)-subspace grids. This is the one remaining general implication on the line.

The next step writes the characteristic-\(p\) versions of the coefficient formula for \(\mathbb F_p\)-subspaces, of lemma (a), of the independence and sharpness lemmas, and of the principle, and checks them against the recorded \(q=3,5\) data. If that goes through, the line closes with a single counting theorem for all additive grids.

The counting proof of the binary lower bound is a candidate for the preprint, as is a Lean formalization of it. Both are the user's decisions.

25 September 2026 — The root-curve theory in every characteristic, and a counting proof of the q-ary lower bound

Working proof: the stable law and counting principle for F_p-subspace grids in characteristic p. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The route review named one remaining general implication on the line. Do the counting principle and the stable per-order law hold in characteristic \(p\), for \(\mathbb F_p\)-subspace grids? If so, they give a counting proof of the \(q\)-ary lower bound for every prime power.

Setting. \(F\) is a field of characteristic \(p\), and \(S\subseteq F\) is an \(\mathbb F_p\)-subspace of size \(s=p^\sigma\). Then \(L=\prod_{t\in S}(x-t)=\sum_{i\le\sigma}c_ix^{p^i}\) is additive, with \(c_\sigma=1\) and \(c_0=L'(0)=\prod_{t\ne0}(-t)\ne0\). The additive root \(x(y)=\sum_ua_uy^{p^u}\) of \(L(x)=y\) has \(a_0=c_0^{-1}\) and \(c_0a_u=-\sum_{1\le i\le\min(u,\sigma)}c_ia_{u-i}^{p^i}\). Everything else is as in characteristic 2: \(y_i=L(x_i)\), \(z_i=x(y_iT)\), \(K=F(y)\), \(\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)\), \(N_{s,n}(d)\), \(\kappa\), and the savings \(h=n(s-1)+s(k-1)-\delta_S\). Multiplicity is Hasse multiplicity, \(P\in\mathfrak m_t^k\), as in the \(q\)-ary theorem.

The theorem

Working theorem (thm:root-curve-theory-char-p). In this setting, for all \(n\ge1\):

  1. the coefficient formula holds with \(\Lambda_\varepsilon\) the \(X^\varepsilon\)-coefficient of \(c_0^{-1}L(X-x)/(X-x)\);
  2. lemma (a) holds: savings \(\ge h\) at \(m\) force \(m\notin\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(h-1)\);
  3. \(|\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)|=N_{s,n}(d)\);
  4. the counting principle holds;
  5. the stable per-order law holds: \(h\le\kappa(m)\), \(h\) does not decrease in \(\ell\), \(h=\kappa(m)\) for large \(\ell\), and \(\#\{\kappa\le d\}=N_{s,n}(d)\).

Proof. The characteristic-2 proofs apply line by line. These are the only places where the characteristic appears, with what replaces each.

  • Facts (a)–(c) of the coefficient formula. \(L\) is monic of degree \(s\), and \(x_i^s=y_i-\sum_{i'<\sigma}c_{i'}x_i^{p^{i'}}\) never lowers a \(y\)-exponent. The CRT count is unchanged.
  • Lagrange. The nodes \(x(y)+t\) differ by the units \(t-t'\). The basis polynomial for the node \(x(y)\) is \(\prod_{t\ne0}(X-x-t)/(-t)=c_0^{-1}L(X-x)/(X-x)\), since \(L(X-x)=\prod_t(X-x-t)\) and \(\prod_{t\ne0}(-t)=c_0\).
  • Triangularity. \(L(Z)/Z=Z^{s-1}+\sum_{1\le i<\sigma}c_iZ^{p^i-1}+c_0\). With \(Z=X-x\), the \(X^{s-1-a}\)-coefficient is \(\binom{s-1}{a}(-x)^a\) plus lower powers \(x^{p^i-s+a}\). Since \(s-1\) has all base-\(p\) digits \(p-1\), Lucas' theorem gives \(\binom{s-1}{a}\not\equiv0\pmod p\). The signs are constants. So the condition system, both directions, and lemma (a) carry over.
  • Reduction. \(z_i^s=y_iT-\sum_{j<\sigma}c_jz_i^{p^j}\), with the same cost bookkeeping.
  • Independence. The conjugates of \(z_j\) over \(E_{j-1}\) are \(z_j+S'\) for an additive subgroup \(S'\le S\), which is automatically an \(\mathbb F_p\)-subspace. The minimal polynomial is \(L_{S'}(Z)-L_{S'}(z_j)\). \(L=L_2\circ L_{S'}\), where \(L_2\) is the subspace polynomial of \(L_{S'}(S)\), of degree \(s/|S'|\ge p\ge2\) when \(S'\ne S\). The degree argument in \(y_j\) and the transcendence of \(y_j\) over \(k((T))\) do not use the characteristic.
  • Sharpness. Use \(G_i=y_1L(Z_i)-y_iL(Z_1)\) and \(L=Z^s+\lambda\) with \(\deg\lambda\le s/p\). Then \(Z^e=y_1^{-1}Z^{e-s\epsilon_i}\bigl(G_i-y_1\lambda(Z_i)+y_iZ_1^s+y_i\lambda(Z_1)\bigr)\), and the same induction applies.
  • Principle and stable law. These use only (1)–(3), the multiplication by \(L(x_1)\) (simple zeros at all of \(S\), and lowest form \(c_0x_1\)), and linear algebra over \(K\). \(\square\)

The \(q\)-ary lower bound by counting

Working corollary (cor:q-ary-counting-proof). For every prime power \(q\), the lower bound of the \(q\)-ary theorem follows from its construction by the counting principle.

Proof. Take \(S=\mathbb F_q\subseteq F\), so \(L=x^q-x\), \(s=q\), and the grid is \(\mathbb F_q^n\).

  • Condition (1). The construction \((x_1^q-x_1)^\ell F^{qQ}P(1)\) attains savings \(H(m)=qQ+s_q(r)\), \(m=Qq^n+r\), for every \(\ell\).
  • Condition (2). The base-\(q\) digits give a bijection \(m\mapsto(Q,r_0,\ldots,r_{n-1})\) with \(H(m)=qQ+\sum r_j\). So \(\#\{H\le d\}=N_{q,n}(d)\), and the principle gives \(h\le H(m)\) for every \(\ell\).

This does not use steps 2–5 of the original proof: the multi-additive forms, the multinomial vanishing modulo \(p\), and the subspace reduction. \(\square\)

Checks. research/tools/bmd_root_curve_orders_p.py builds \(\mathbb F_{p^a}\) from a primitive polynomial and computes \(\mathrm{Ord}(d)\) on the root curve of \(x^q-x\) at random \(y\). It compares the result with \(\{m:H(m)\le d\}\) for the base-\(q\) law. All comparisons are equal, in two trials each:

  • \(q=3\), \(n=2\), \(d\le6\), in \(\mathbb F_{243}\);
  • \(q=3\), \(n=3\), \(d\le4\), in \(\mathbb F_{243}\);
  • \(q=5\), \(n=2\), \(d\le5\), in \(\mathbb F_{125}\);
  • \(q=9\), \(n=2\), \(d\le4\), in \(\mathbb F_{729}\).

Outputs are in research/results/bmd-r21-char-p/. The recorded kernel values for \(q=3,5\) (check:q-ary-per-order-values) agree with the \(q\)-ary theorem, which this corollary reproves.

General statement. thm:root-curve-theory-char-p, for every prime \(p\), every \(\mathbb F_p\)-subspace \(S\) of a field of characteristic \(p\), all \(n\ge1\) and all \(0\le\ell<k\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the coefficient formula, the \(n\)-variable lemma, the sharpness lemma, the stable law, and the \(q\)-ary theorem with its construction. Claim searches (characteristic \(p\), root curve, counting) found only this thread's characteristic-2 versions. A fresh-context referee checked every replacement listed above. It also read the characteristic-2 proofs of lemma (a), the principle and the stable law for hidden uses of \(1=-1\) or of parity, and found none beyond those listed. It found no error. Its own script over \(\mathbb F_{3^5}\) (a different modulus, three random points, \(d\le4\)) reproduced \(\mathrm{Ord}(d)=\{m:H(m)\le d\}\) in all 15 cases. Its optional clarity edits are made: Hasse multiplicity is named, and the field of each check is stated.

Assessment. The grid-extension line is closed in its general form. On every additive grid in every characteristic, the per-order savings stabilize at the root-curve orders, with the universal count \(N_{s,n}\). The exact laws on full grids, in every dimension, come from their constructions by counting. What remains is arithmetic, namely \(\kappa\) for particular \(S\) and the threshold \(\ell_0\), and it does not bear on the thread's goal. For the preprint, the counting proofs replace the longest part of the per-order argument. Whether to use them, and whether to formalize them in Lean, are the user's decisions. The next research step should return to the thread's own route and ask what remains open there.

25 September 2026 — The stable per-order law on every finite grid, and the odd-characteristic cube explained

Working proof: the root-curve stable law for every finite grid S^n over every field. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The odd-characteristic cube was deprioritized for lack of a mechanism. The binary laws fail there from \(n=3\), and the local root of \(X^2-X=y\) is a Catalan series, not additive. The root-curve argument of the stable per-order law used additivity in three places. Can it do without?

Setting. \(F\) is any field, and \(S\subseteq F\) is finite with \(0\in S\) and \(|S|=s\ge2\). Put \(L=\prod_{t\in S}(x-t)\), so \(L(0)=0\) and \(L'(0)=\prod_{t\ne0}(-t)\ne0\), and \(y_i=L(x_i)\). For each \(t\in S\), let \(r_t(y)\in F[[y]]\) be the root of \(L(X)=y\) with \(r_t(0)=t\); it exists by Hensel, since the roots of \(L\) are simple. Put \(z_i=r_0(y_iT)\), so \(L(z_i)=y_iT\). \(\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(d)\), \(N_{s,n}(d)\), \(\kappa\) and the savings \(h=n(s-1)+s(k-1)-\delta_S(n,k,\ell)\) are as before, with Hasse multiplicity.

The theorem

Working theorem (thm:finite-grid-stable-law). In this setting, for all \(n\ge1\) and \(m\ge0\):

  • \(h(k,\ell)\le\kappa(m)\) for every \(\ell\) (with \(k=\ell+m+1\));
  • \(h\) does not decrease in \(\ell\), and \(h=\kappa(m)\) for all large \(\ell\);
  • \(\#\{m:\kappa(m)\le d\}=N_{s,n}(d)\).

The counting principle holds as well: an attained law with count \(N_{s,n}\) equals \(\kappa\). Hence it bounds the savings at every \(\ell\), with equality from the attaining \(\ell\) on.

Proof. Follow the coefficient formula, the \(n\)-variable lemma, the sharpness lemma and the stable law, with three changes.

  1. Nodes. Facts (a)–(c) need only \(L\) monic of degree \(s\) with simple roots. For (b), the dimension count holds because the points are distinct and \((y)^k\subseteq\bigcap_a\mathfrak m_a^k\). For (c), the admissible polynomial of a jet exists by CRT: take \(g\equiv1\) modulo \(x^k\) and \(g\equiv0\) modulo \((x-t)^k\) for \(t\ne0\), and \(g_b=x^bg\). The nodes are the \(r_t(y)\), and \(r_t-r_{t'}\equiv t-t'\) modulo \(y\) is a unit. At a node \(t\ne0\), \(P(v)(r_t(y))\in(y)^k\), because \(r_t-t\in(y)\). At the node \(t=0\) it is \(v(r_0(y))\) modulo \((y)^k\).
  2. Lagrange basis and triangularity. \(\prod_t(X-r_t)=L(X)-y\), so the basis polynomial for \(r_0\) is \(\dfrac{L(X)-L(r_0)}{(X-r_0)\,L'(r_0)}\).
    • Write \(L=\sum_j\ell_jX^j\). Then \((L(X)-L(r_0))/(X-r_0)=\sum_j\ell_j\sum_{i<j}X^ir_0^{j-1-i}\). Its \(X^{s-1-a}\)-coefficient is \(r_0^a\) plus lower powers of \(r_0\), since \(\ell_s=1\).
    • So \(\Lambda_{s-1-a}=(r_0^a+\text{lower})/L'(r_0)\), where \(L'(r_0)\) is a unit series.
    • Put \(\tilde w=w/\prod_iL'(r_0(y_i))\), with \(w=v(r_0(y))\). Then \(v\mapsto\tilde w\) is an order-preserving bijection on truncated series.
    • The coefficient conditions become the triangular system in \(\tilde w\) and the powers \(r_0(y_i)^{a_i}\), exactly as before, in both directions. Lemma (a) and the converse in the stable law's part (3) then go through verbatim. The reduction is \(z_i^s=y_iT-(L-X^s)(z_i)\).
  3. Independence without additivity. Over \(E_{j-1}=E'(y_j)\), where \(y_j\) is transcendental over \(E'\) (same proof), the polynomial \(L(Z)-y_jT\) is irreducible.
    • In \(E'[y_j,Z]\) it has degree 1 in \(y_j\), with coefficients \(-T\) and \(L(Z)\). In a factorization, the factor free of \(y_j\) divides \(-T\), a unit of \(E'\), so it is a unit.
    • It is monic in \(Z\), hence primitive over \(E'[y_j]\). By Gauss it is irreducible over \(E'(y_j)\), so \([E_j:E_{j-1}]=s\).
    This replaces the coset argument and needs no structure on \(S\).

The sharpness lemma uses only \(G_i=y_1L(Z_i)-y_iL(Z_1)\) and \(\deg(L-X^s)<s\). The multiplication by \(L(x_1)\) needs its simple zeros at all of \(S\) and its lowest form \(L'(0)x_1\). Nothing else in the proofs uses the structure of \(S\) or additivity of \(L\). \(\square\)

The odd-characteristic cube

For \(S=\{0,1\}\), \(L=x^2-x\), \(r_0(y)=\sum_{j\ge1}(-1)^jC_{j-1}y^j\) (Catalan numbers; \(r_0=r_0^2-y\)). The theorem says that the per-order savings on \(\{0,1\}^n\) over any field stabilize at the orders of \(z_i=r_0(y_iT)\), with exactly \(N_{2,n}(d)\) values of \(\kappa\) at most \(d\). This is Menezes' setting (arXiv 2609.19009), and the coefficients of \(r_0\) are, up to sign, the Catalan numbers of his formula. Whether \(\kappa\) reproduces his \(\rho_F\) is open.

Test against the recorded data.

  • research/tools/bmd_root_curve_orders_grid.py computes \(\mathrm{Ord}(d)\) for arbitrary \(S\) over \(\mathbb F_{p^a}\), at random \(y\), with the root found by fixed-point iteration. Over \(\mathbb F_{243}\) (\(p=3\)) and \(\mathbb F_{125}\) (\(p=5\)), \(n=2,\ldots,5\), every trial has \(|\mathrm{Ord}(d)|=N_{2,n}(d)\).
  • bmd_kappa_compare.py compares \(\kappa\) with the recorded kernel values (research/results/bmd-r9-odd-cube/, \(k\le13\)). In all eight cases no savings exceed \(\kappa(m)\).
  • The largest computed \(\ell\) attains \(\kappa(m)\) for every \(m\), except \(m=12\) at \(p=3\), \(n=3,4\), where only \(\ell=0\) was computed, and \(m=10\) at \(p=5\), \(n=5\), where only \(\ell=0,1\) were computed.
  • \(\kappa\) is computed at random points of \(\mathbb F_{243}\) and \(\mathbb F_{125}\), so it is a specialization. A matching count does not prove a specialization generic. The evidence is that both trials agree on every compared \(m\); they differ only beyond that range (at \(p=5\), \(n=3\), \(d=5\)). The kernel data are over \(\mathbb F_3\) and \(\mathbb F_5\). For \(S=\{0,1\}\), \(r_0\) has coefficients in \(\mathbb F_p\), so \(\kappa\) over \(\mathbb F_p(y)\) equals \(\kappa\) over \(F(y)\) for any \(F\supseteq\mathbb F_p\).
  • \(\kappa\) reproduces the non-monotone values over \(\mathbb F_5\): \(\kappa(4)=2\) but \(\kappa(5)=1\) at \(n=4\).

The exchange-law failure explained. The exchange law \(\delta(n,k,\ell)=2\ell+\delta(n,k-\ell,0)\) says exactly that the savings at \(\ell\) equal those at \(\ell=0\) for the same \(m\). At \(n=3\), \(\kappa(8)=3\) over both fields. The recorded savings at \(m=8\) are \(2,2,2,3,3\) for \(\ell=0,\ldots,4\) over \(\mathbb F_3\), and \(2,2,2,3\) for \(\ell=0,\ldots,3\) over \(\mathbb F_5\). So \(\delta(3,12,3)=22\) is the stable value, reached at \(\ell=3\), and the exchange law fails because \(\ell=0\) has not yet stabilized. Over \(\mathbb F_2\) the binary theorem has savings depending only on \(m\), so it is stable from \(\ell=0\). The odd cube stabilizes later.

General statement. thm:finite-grid-stable-law, for every field, every finite \(S\ni0\), all \(n\ge1\) and \(m\ge0\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the additive-case sharpness lemma, stable law, \(n\)-variable lemma and coefficient formula with its facts. Claim searches (finite grid, root curve, cube, Catalan, Menezes) found Menezes' imported theorem and this thread's additive versions. A fresh-context referee checked the three changes (Hensel nodes; the Lagrange basis with \(\prod_{t\ne0}(r_0-r_t)=L'(r_0)\); absorbing the unit, in both directions, with the converse's homogeneity argument on \(\tilde w\); independence by Gauss), the remaining steps, the Catalan signs and the data claims. It found no mathematical gap. It asked for these corrections, all made: the counting-principle sentence, which as first written claimed equality at every \(\ell\) and was false; the \(\mathbb F_5\) data at \(m=8\); the explicit CRT lift in fact (c); the specialization caveat; the Menezes remark; and a qualified assessment.

Assessment. The odd-characteristic route item has a mechanism for the stable values: beyond the dimension-two theorem, the stable per-order law on the cube in every characteristic is the order function of the Catalan root curve, with a universal count. The theorem says nothing about the threshold \(\ell_0\) or about the small-\(\ell\) values, including \(\ell=0\), where the exchange law fails. What remains is to compute \(\kappa\) for the cube in closed form, and to relate it to Menezes' formula in his range \(n\ge k-1\), where he determines every \(\ell\). A natural conjecture is that \(\kappa\) there is \(\rho_F\)-type in \(m\) and \(n\), which would reprove his lower bound by counting. The next step tests that.

25 September 2026 — The cube over every field: a capped Catalan lower bound for all n, and Menezes' formula for n ≥ k − ℓ − 1 (conditional)

Working proof of the lower bound for all n; the extension of Menezes' range is conditional on his construction. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. On the cube \(\{0,1\}^n\), how do the root-curve orders \(\kappa_n(m)\) of the finite-grid stable law relate to Menezes' formula \(\delta=n+2k-2-\rho_F(k-\ell)\)? That formula is imported and holds for \(n\ge k-1\) (imp:menezes-characteristic-drop-degree). Here \(\rho_F(c)\) is the least number of parts \(a\ge1\) with \(C_{a-1}\ne0\) in \(F\) summing to \(c-1\). Put \(\nu_F(a)=\#\{1\le j\le a:C_{j-1}\ne0\text{ in }F\}\). Call a part \(a\) \(n\)-allowed if \(C_{a-1}\ne0\) and \(\nu_F(a)\le n\), and let \(\rho_{F,\le n}(c)\) be the least number of \(n\)-allowed parts summing to \(c-1\). Every allowed \(a\le n\) is \(n\)-allowed, since \(\nu_F(a)\le a\).

Setting. \(S=\{0,1\}\) and \(L=x^2-x\). The root is \(r_0(y)=\sum_{j\ge1}(-1)^jC_{j-1}y^j\), and \(z_i=r_0(y_iT)\) over \(K=F(y_1,\ldots,y_n)\). Savings are \(h=n+2(k-1)-\delta_F(n,k,\ell)\), and \(m=k-\ell-1\).

Catalan linear forms

Working lemma (lem:catalan-linear-forms). If \(a\) is \(n\)-allowed, some \(K\)-linear form in \(z_1,\ldots,z_n\) has \(T\)-order exactly \(a\). Hence \(m\in\mathrm{Ord}_{S,n}(\rho_{F,\le n}(m+1))\), that is, \(\kappa_n(m)\le\rho_{F,\le n}(m+1)\).

Proof.

  • One form. The \(T^j\)-coefficient of \(\sum_i\beta_iz_i\) is \((-1)^jC_{j-1}\sum_i\beta_iy_i^j\). It vanishes automatically when \(C_{j-1}=0\). Let \(J=\{1\le j\le a:C_{j-1}\ne0\}\), with \(|J|=\nu_F(a)=:\nu\le n\). The generalized Vandermonde matrix \((y_i^j)_{i\le\nu,\,j\in J}\) has nonzero determinant in \(K\): it is a nonzero polynomial in independent variables, since its expansion has distinct monomials. So there are \(\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_\nu\) with \(\sum_i\beta_iy_i^j=[j=a]\) for \(j\in J\). The form then has zero coefficients below \(T^a\), and \((-1)^aC_{a-1}\ne0\) at \(T^a\).
  • Products. If \(m=a_1+\cdots+a_r\) with allowed parts, the product of the \(r\) forms has degree \(r\) in \(z\) and order exactly \(m\), since \(K[[T]]\) is a domain. \(\square\)

The capped Catalan bound

Working theorem (thm:cube-capped-catalan-bound). For every field \(F\), all \(n\ge1\) and \(0\le\ell<k\),

\[ \delta_F(n,k,\ell)\ \ge\ n+2(k-1)-\rho_{F,\le n}(k-\ell). \]

When \(n\ge k-\ell-1\) the cap does not bind, and the right side is \(n+2k-2-\rho_F(k-\ell)\).

Proof. By the finite-grid stable law, part (1), \(h\le\kappa_n(m)\) for every \(\ell\). By the lemma, \(\kappa_n(m)\le\rho_{F,\le n}(m+1)\). If \(n\ge m\), every allowed part \(a\le m\) is \(n\)-allowed, so \(\rho_{F,\le n}(m+1)=\rho_F(m+1)\). \(\square\)

  • Menezes' lower bound. For \(n\ge k-1\ge m\) this is his lower bound, proved here without his argument, which was not read.
  • Characteristic 0. Every part is allowed and \(\nu(a)=a\), so \(\rho_{F,\le n}(c)=\lceil(c-1)/n\rceil\), and \(\delta\ge n+2(k-1)-\lceil(k-\ell-1)/n\rceil\) for all \(n\). With \(\ell\le k-2\) and \(n\ge k-\ell-1\), this is the Sauermann–Wigderson bound \(n+2k-3\). For all \(\ell\le k-2\) at once it needs \(n\ge k-1\): Menezes' range, and a weaker hypothesis than their \(n\ge2k-3\), since \(k-1\le2k-3\).
  • Characteristic 2. \(C_j\) is odd exactly when \(j+1\) is a power of 2, and \(\nu(2^i)=i+1\). So the \(n\)-allowed parts are \(1,2,4,\ldots,2^{n-1}\). For parts that are powers of 2 the greedy decomposition is optimal. Write \(m=Q2^n+r\) with \(r<2^n\). Then \(m\) splits into \(2Q+\lfloor r/2^{n-1}\rfloor\) parts \(2^{n-1}\) plus the binary digits of \(r\bmod2^{n-1}\), so \(\rho_{\mathbb F_2,\le n}(m+1)=2Q+s_2(r)\). This is exactly the savings of the binary theorem. So the capped bound gives a second new proof of the binary per-order lower bound in every dimension, after the counting proof (cor:root-curve-lower-bounds). Unlike that proof, it needs no upper-bound construction: only the stable law's part (1), the Vandermonde forms and the parity of Catalan numbers.

Menezes' formula for \(n\ge k-\ell-1\) (conditional)

Conditional corollary (cor:menezes-range-extension). Assume Menezes' upper bound at origin order 0: \(\delta_F(n,c,0)\le n+2c-2-\rho_F(c)\) for \(c\ge2\) and \(n\ge c-1\). (His theorem assumes \(k\ge2\). For \(c=1\) the bound \(n\) is attained by \(\prod_i(1-x_i)\), which vanishes at every nonzero cube point and is 1 at the origin.) Then \(\delta_F(n,k,\ell)=n+2k-2-\rho_F(k-\ell)\) for all \(n\ge k-\ell-1\), not only for \(n\ge k-1\).

Proof. Multiply his order-zero polynomial for \(c=k-\ell\) by \(L(x_1)^\ell\). This adds \(\ell\) to the multiplicity at every cube point, giving at least \((k-\ell)+\ell=k\), and gives origin order exactly \(\ell\), with lowest form \((-x_1)^\ell\) times a nonzero constant. The degree is \(2\ell+n+2(k-\ell)-2-\rho_F(k-\ell)\). The theorem gives the matching lower bound. \(\square\)

His upper bound is an explicit construction (arXiv 2609.19009, Theorem 1.1). This thread has not read or verified it, so the corollary is conditional. Over \(\mathbb F_2\) it holds unconditionally, by the binary theorem.

Checks. research/tools/bmd_cube_rho_cap_check.py reads all recorded cube values (research/results/bmd-r9-odd-cube/).

  • Over \(\mathbb F_3\): 455 values, none above \(\rho_{\le n}\). All 225 values with \(n\ge m\) are equalities, and 427 of the 455 are equalities overall.
  • Over \(\mathbb F_5\): 390 values, none above \(\rho_{\le n}\). All 205 values with \(n\ge m\) are equalities, and 366 of the 390 overall.
  • All 170 values over \(\mathbb F_3\) and all 150 over \(\mathbb F_5\) outside Menezes' range, with \(n<k-1\le n+\ell\), are equalities (rho-cap-p3.txt, rho-cap-p5.txt).

The root-curve computation at \(n=10\) over \(\mathbb F_3\), at two random specializations \(y\in\mathbb F_{3^5}^{10}\) (not generically), with \(d\le2\) and \(T\) truncated at 40, gives \(\kappa_{10}(m)=\rho_{\mathbb F_3}(m+1)\) for all \(m\le9\). For example \(\kappa_{10}(9)=1\), because \(C_8=1430\not\equiv0\) modulo 3. Outputs are in research/results/bmd-r23-menezes-kappa/.

General statement. thm:cube-capped-catalan-bound, for every field, all \(n\ge1\) and \(0\le\ell<k\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the finite-grid stable law, the imported statement of Menezes' theorem (including its hypothesis \(k\ge2\)), and the binary theorem. Claim searches (Catalan, Menezes, Sauermann–Wigderson, cube lower bound) found the imported theorem and this thread's results. A fresh-context referee checked the root, the Vandermonde step, the theorem, the corollary's degrees, the characteristic-0 and characteristic-2 claims (by script: Catalan parity and \(\nu(2^i)=i+1\) to 256, and the greedy identity against the binary theorem for \(n\le7\), \(m<250\)), and all quoted counts. It found no mathematical error in the lemma or the theorem. Its required changes are made: the multiplicity added by \(L(x_1)^\ell\), which the draft wrongly gave as 1; the case \(c=1\) outside Menezes' hypothesis; committed provenance for all counts; the specialization caveat at \(n=10\); the link to the earlier counting proof; clearer range wording; and the qualified assessment.

Assessment. On the cube, given the finite-grid stable law, which carries the weight, Menezes' lower bound becomes a two-line Vandermonde argument, and extends it to every \(n\) with capped parts. His formula then covers \(n\ge k-\ell-1\), conditional on his construction. Below that range, \(\kappa_n\) can be strictly smaller than \(\rho_{F,\le n}\): at \(n=3\) over \(\mathbb F_3\), \(\kappa(7)=2\), but \(\rho_{\le3}(8)=3\), since the 3-allowed parts are \(1,2,3\). With \(n=3\) over \(\mathbb F_3\), a linear form in \(z\) has order at most 3, since order \(a\ge4\) needs \(\nu(a)-1\ge3\) independent conditions on 3 unknowns. So products of at most two affine forms have order at most 6, and degree-2 polynomials that reach order 7 must be genuinely nonlinear. The refined count \(\nu_F\) already explains the \(\mathbb F_5\), \(n=4\) value \(\kappa(5)=1\). The next step is to find the degree-2 polynomials behind \(\kappa_3(7)=2\) over \(\mathbb F_3\), and their general pattern.

25 September 2026 — The Catalan sandwich: Menezes' formula for n ≥ k − ℓ − 1, unconditionally, over every field

Working proof: ρ_F(k−ℓ) ≤ savings ≤ ρ_{F,≤n}(k−ℓ) on the cube over every field. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The range extension rested on Menezes' order-zero construction, which was imported but not read. His Theorem 1.1 construction is \(y^d\hat g_{k-|d|}\), with his Catalan truncation \(\hat g_s\) (arXiv 2609.19009, read this cycle from the HTML statement and construction). This thread proved its properties only over \(\mathbb F_2\) (truncation lemma). Does a short proof work over every field and in every dimension?

Setting. \(F\) is any field, \(y_i=x_i^2-x_i\), and \(C_j\) are the Catalan numbers. \(\rho_F\) and \(\rho_{F,\le n}\) are as in the capped Catalan bound, and the savings are \(h=n+2(k-1)-\delta_F(n,k,\ell)\).

The Catalan truncation over every field

Working theorem (thm:catalan-truncation-any-field). Put \(N_s(x)=(x-1)-\sum_{a=1}^{s-1}(-1)^{a-1}C_{a-1}y^a\), with \(y=x^2-x\). Expand \(\prod_{i=1}^nN_s(x_i)\) over the subsets \(S\subseteq[n]\) and exponents \(a_i\ge1\) (\(i\in S\)). Let \(g_s\) be the sum of the terms with \(\sum_{i\in S}a_i\le s-1\). Then for all \(n,s\ge1\):

  • \(g_s\) has multiplicity \(\ge s\) at every nonzero cube point, and \(g_s(0)=(-1)^n\);
  • \(\deg g_s\le n+2s-2-\rho_F(s)\).

Hence \(P=y_1^\ell g_{k-\ell}\) has multiplicity \(\ge k\) at nonzero points, origin order exactly \(\ell\), and degree \(\le n+2k-2-\rho_F(k-\ell)\), in every dimension.

Proof.

  • Vanishing of \(N_s\). Put \(u=x-1\), so \(y=u+u^2\). The Catalan series \(u(y)=\sum_{a\ge1}(-1)^{a-1}C_{a-1}y^a\) solves \(u+u^2=y\): it is \(-c(-y)\), where \(c=t+c^2\) is the generating function \(\sum C_{a-1}t^a\). It has integer coefficients, so it is valid in every characteristic. Substituting \(y=u+u^2\) gives back \(u\): \(w=u(y(u))\) satisfies \(w+w^2=u+u^2\), so \((w-u)(w+u+1)=0\), and \(w+u+1\) is a unit. So the difference \(u-\sum_{a<s}(\cdots)y^a=\sum_{a\ge s}(\cdots)y^a\) has order \(\ge s\) in \(u\). So \((x-1)^s\mid N_s\) as polynomials, since \(N_s\) is a polynomial whose expansion at \(x=1\) begins in degree \(\ge s\).
  • Multiplicity. \(\prod_iN_s(x_i)=g_s+R\), where \(R\) collects the terms with \(\sum a_i\ge s\).
    • At a nonzero cube point some coordinate is 1, so the product has multiplicity \(\ge s\).
    • Each \(y_i\) vanishes at \(x_i\in\{0,1\}\), so a term with \(\prod_{i\in S}y_i^{a_i}\) has multiplicity \(\ge\sum a_i\ge s\) at every cube point.
    • So \(g_s\) has multiplicity \(\ge s\) at nonzero points. At the origin only \(S=\emptyset\) survives, giving \(\prod_j(0-1)=(-1)^n\).
  • Degree. A term \((S,a)\) with nonzero coefficient has every \(C_{a_i-1}\ne0\) in \(F\), and degree \(2\sigma+n-|S|\) with \(\sigma=\sum a_i\le s-1\). So \(|S|\ge\rho_F(\sigma+1)\), which is finite since part 1 is always allowed (\(C_0=1\)), with \(\rho_F(c)\le c-1\). Since \(\rho_F(\sigma+2)\le\rho_F(\sigma+1)+1\) (part 1 is always allowed), \(\sigma\mapsto2\sigma-\rho_F(\sigma+1)\) is strictly increasing. So every term has degree \(\le n+2(s-1)-\rho_F(s)\).
  • \(P\). \(y_1\) vanishes simply at every cube point and has lowest form \(-x_1\) at the origin. So \(P\) has multiplicity \(\ge\ell+(k-\ell)\) off the origin and order exactly \(\ell\) at it. Its degree is \(2\ell+\deg g_{k-\ell}\). \(\square\)

Over \(\mathbb F_2\) this is the truncation lemma up to signs, since \(C_{a-1}\) is odd exactly for powers of 2. The construction is Menezes'; the proof above uses only the Catalan series and needs no condition on \(n\).

The Catalan sandwich

Working theorem (thm:cube-catalan-sandwich). For every field \(F\), all \(n\ge1\) and \(0\le\ell<k\), with \(m=k-\ell-1\),

\[ \rho_F(m+1)\ \le\ h\ \le\ \kappa_n(m)\ \le\ \rho_{F,\le n}(m+1). \]

In particular \(\delta_F(n,k,\ell)=n+2k-2-\rho_F(k-\ell)\) whenever \(n\ge k-\ell-1\). This extends Menezes' formula from \(n\ge k-1\) to \(n\ge k-\ell-1\), unconditionally.

Proof. The left inequality is the theorem above. The middle one is part (1) of the finite-grid stable law, and the right one is the Catalan linear-form lemma behind the capped Catalan bound. For \(n\ge m\) the outer sides agree. \(\square\)

This makes cor:menezes-range-extension a working proof. imp:menezes-characteristic-drop-degree is reproved with independent proofs: the construction is his, the proof of its properties here is new, and the lower half rests on this thread's finite-grid stable law, part (1). His Section 8 remark that the hypothesis \(n\ge k-1\) is substantial, because basis elements cancel, concerns sharpness as we read it. The single polynomial \(y_1^\ell g_{k-\ell}\) is an upper bound for every \(n\), and the sandwich shows sharpness for \(n\ge k-\ell-1\). The outer bounds differ only when some part of an optimal decomposition exceeds the cap. There \(h\) lies between \(\rho_F(m+1)\) and \(\kappa_n(m)\), and it equals \(\kappa_n(m)\) for large \(\ell\).

Checks.

  • The construction itself. research/tools/bmd_catalan_truncation_check.py builds \(P=y_1^\ell g_{k-\ell}\) exactly over \(\mathbb F_p\). It computes Hasse multiplicities by shifting to every cube point, and checks multiplicity, exact origin order and degree:
    • \(p=3\): 84 cases (\(n\le3\), \(k\le7\));
    • \(p=5\): 84 cases (\(n\le3\), \(k\le7\));
    • \(p=7\): 63 cases (\(n\le3\), \(k\le6\)).
    There are no failures. A negative control with the Catalan signs flipped (--wrong-sign) fails 20 of 30 cases at \(p=3\) (truncation-control-p3.txt).
  • The sandwich. bmd_cube_rho_cap_check.py, extended to report the uncapped side, finds all 845 recorded values over \(\mathbb F_3\) and \(\mathbb F_5\) at least \(\rho_F(m+1)\), and at most \(\rho_{F,\le n}(m+1)\) (sandwich-p3.txt, sandwich-p5.txt). Of these, 178 of 455 over \(\mathbb F_3\) and 141 of 390 over \(\mathbb F_5\) lie strictly above \(\rho_F(m+1)\).

Outputs are in research/results/bmd-r24-cube/.

General statement. thm:cube-catalan-sandwich, for every field, all \(n\ge1\) and \(0\le\ell<k\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: Menezes' Theorem 1.1 statement and construction (arXiv HTML), the \(\mathbb F_2\) truncation lemma, the capped Catalan bound and the finite-grid stable law. Claim searches (Catalan truncation, Menezes, cube) found the imported theorem and this thread's \(\mathbb F_2\) version. A fresh-context referee checked the Catalan series and the divisibility over \(\mathbb Z\), the multiplicity split, the degree bound (vacuous when \(n<\rho_F(\sigma+1)\)), \(P\), the sandwich over Menezes' whole range including \(\ell=k-1\), and the check script, and scanned the data independently. It found both theorems correct. Its required changes are made: a wrong "smallest instance" of the gap, the sharper chain through \(\kappa_n\), committed generators for the sandwich check and the negative control, the line \(u(y(u))=u\), the finiteness of \(\rho_F\), the reading of Menezes' Section 8 remark, and the credit wording.

Assessment. On the cube, the per-order problem is solved for \(n\ge k-\ell-1\) over every field, with short proofs of both bounds. Below that range, \(h\) lies between \(\rho_F(m+1)\) and \(\kappa_n(m)\), and the data show both ends occurring. The outer bounds first separate at \(n=1\), \(m=2\), where \(\rho_F(3)=1\) but the one-variable value \(h=m=2\) sits at the upper end. At \(n=3\) over \(\mathbb F_3\), \(m=7\), the value is already determined, \(h=2\), because \(\kappa_3(7)=2=\rho_{\mathbb F_3}(8)\). The route item's remaining question is where \(h\) lies in this interval, and at which \(\ell\) it reaches \(\kappa\). Since the thread's goal is settled and this line now reproves and extends the one external result it depends on, a route review is due next.

25 September 2026 — Route review of the cube over every field: settled for n ≥ k − ℓ − 1, sandwiched below

Route review; the Catalan sandwich passes a test on a field not used in its development. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

  • The line's general claim. Determine \(\delta_F(n,k,\ell)\) on the cube \(\{0,1\}^n\) over fields of characteristic \(\ne2\), below Menezes' range \(n\ge k-1\).
  • What is established.
    • Dimension two over every field (square theorem).
    • The stable law with the universal count, on every finite grid (finite-grid stable law).
    • The sandwich \(\rho_F(m+1)\le h\le\kappa_n(m)\le\rho_{F,\le n}(m+1)\) (Catalan sandwich). It gives Menezes' formula for all \(n\ge k-\ell-1\), unconditionally, with independent proofs of both bounds.
  • What the goal needs. Nothing: the thread's goal is \(D(n,k)\) over \(\mathbb F_2\), which is proved and formalized. The line matters for the preprint's context, since it reproves and extends the one external result the thread builds on.

Falsification attempt: a new field

The sandwich was developed against the \(\mathbb F_3\) and \(\mathbb F_5\) data. As an independent test, the cube kernel (bmd_jet_orders_q --cube) computed all per-order values over \(\mathbb F_7\) for \(n\le4\), \(k\le11\): 264 values, in research/results/bmd-r25-cube-review/p7/. The prediction, fixed before the run, was \(\rho_{\mathbb F_7}(m+1)\le h\le\rho_{\mathbb F_7,\le n}(m+1)\), with equality whenever \(n\ge m\). Over \(\mathbb F_7\) the Catalan numbers \(C_0,\ldots,C_5=1,1,2,5,14,42\) have \(14\equiv42\equiv0\). So the parts 5 and 6 are forbidden, which makes this a different pattern from \(\mathbb F_3\) and \(\mathbb F_5\).

Result. bmd_cube_rho_cap_check.py (sandwich-p7.txt):

  • no value lies outside the sandwich;
  • all 134 values with \(n\ge m\) are equalities, including all 100 outside Menezes' range;
  • 253 of 264 values equal the upper end \(\rho_{\le n}\);
  • 108 lie strictly above \(\rho_F\);
  • the 30 values in Menezes' own range match his formula.

The test could have failed at any value below \(\rho_F\), above \(\rho_{\le n}\), or off the formula for \(n\ge m\). None did.

Assessment and next step

The cube line is in the same state as the grid line. Both have:

  • exact values wherever the two Catalan counts agree;
  • a structural description, the root-curve \(\kappa_n\), of the large-\(\ell\) values elsewhere.

What remains is arithmetic. That means \(\kappa_n\) for \(n<m\), where most values sit at the upper end \(\rho_{\le n}\) (253 of 264 over \(\mathbb F_7\)), and the small-\(\ell\) values before stabilization. The observation that the upper end is attained so often suggests a conjecture: \(\kappa_n(m)=\rho_{F,\le n}(m+1)\) except in a sparse set of cases. It should be tested before any work on it. Over \(\mathbb F_3\), \(\kappa_3(7)=2<3=\rho_{\le3}(8)\) is one exception, and it comes from genuinely nonlinear polynomials in \(z\).

No remaining question on either line bears on the thread's goal. The preprint could now contain the following, all worked proofs in this notebook:

  • the \(\mathbb F_2\) per-order theorem, with the one-page counting proof and the Catalan proof of its lower bound;
  • the finite-grid stable law;
  • Menezes' formula extended to \(n\ge k-\ell-1\) over every field.

What goes in is the user's decision. The next research step on the thread's route is to test the exception-set conjecture on the recorded data and on \(\kappa\) at \(n=3,4\). It cannot change the goal, but it is the remaining open structure.

25 September 2026 — The cube's stable orders form initial segments when the first Catalan numbers survive

Conjecture: the initial-segment law for the cube; working proof of its degree-one case and of the hypothesis' necessity. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The route review proposed to test the exception set, where \(\kappa_n(m)<\rho_{F,\le n}(m+1)\). Listing it shows that the capped Catalan count is not the right model.

  • At \(n=3\) the exceptions are the same over \(\mathbb F_3\), \(\mathbb F_5\) and \(\mathbb F_7\): \(m=7,10,11,13,14,15\), with the same \(\kappa\).
  • In all three fields \(\kappa_3(0)=0\) and \(\kappa_3(m)=\lceil(m+1)/4\rceil\) for \(1\le m\le15\), that is \(\kappa_3(m)=\min\{d:4d>m\}\) for \(m\ge1\), independent of the field.

(research/results/bmd-r26-exceptions/exceptions.txt.)

Setting. The cube \(\{0,1\}^n\) over a field \(F\), with the Catalan root curve \(z_i=r_0(y_iT)\), \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)\), \(\kappa_n\) and \(N_{2,n}(d)\) as in the finite-grid stable law, and \(n\)-allowed parts as in the capped Catalan bound.

Degree-one orders

Working lemma (lem:cube-degree-one-orders). \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(1)=\{0\}\cup\{a:a\text{ is }n\text{-allowed}\}\). In particular \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(1)=\{0,1,\ldots,n\}\) if and only if \(C_0,\ldots,C_{n-1}\) are all nonzero in \(F\).

Proof.

  • Constants. A nonzero constant has order 0. A form \(\beta_0+\sum\beta_iz_i\) with \(\beta_0\ne0\) has order 0 too. So the positive orders come from \(\sum_i\beta_iz_i\), whose \(T^j\)-coefficient is \((-1)^jC_{j-1}\sum_i\beta_iy_i^j\).
  • Allowed parts are reached. The linear-form lemma gives every \(n\)-allowed \(a\).
  • Nothing else is reached. Order exactly \(a\) needs \(C_{a-1}\ne0\), and \(\sum_i\beta_iy_i^j=0\) for the \(\nu(a)-1\) exponents \(j<a\) with \(C_{j-1}\ne0\). If \(\nu(a)-1\ge n\), the generalized Vandermonde matrix on \(n\) of those exponents is invertible over \(K\), which forces \(\beta=0\).
  • The segment. There are at least \(n\) allowed parts, by the count \(|\mathrm{Ord}_n(1)|=N_{2,n}(1)=n+1\) of the stable law. The \(n\)-allowed parts are the first \(n\) of them. They are \(1,\ldots,n\) exactly when \(C_0,\ldots,C_{n-1}\ne0\). \(\square\)

The initial-segment law

Conjecture (conj:cube-initial-segment-law). If \(C_0,\ldots,C_{n-1}\) are nonzero in \(F\), in particular in characteristic 0, then \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)=\{0,1,\ldots,N_{2,n}(d)-1\}\) for every \(d\). Equivalently, the stable per-order savings on the cube are

\[ \kappa_n(m)=\min\{d:\ N_{2,n}(d)>m\}. \]

Status and evidence.

  • Proved cases. For \(d\le1\) it is the lemma. For \(n\le2\) it holds in every field. For \(n=1\), \(z_1^j\) has order \(j\), so \(\mathrm{Ord}_1(d)\supseteq\{0,\ldots,d\}\), and the count \(N_{2,1}(d)=d+1\) gives equality. For \(n=2\), \(N_{2,2}(d)=2d+1\) gives \(\lceil m/2\rceil\), which is the square theorem.
  • Tests with the hypothesis. In all 13 computed cases with the hypothesis, the law matches \(\kappa\) at a majority of random specializations, for every \(m\) whose order appears below the computed degree:
    • \(\mathbb F_3\), \(n=2,\ldots,5\);
    • \(\mathbb F_5\), \(n=2,3\);
    • \(\mathbb F_7\), \(n=2,3,4\);
    • \(\mathbb F_{11}\) and \(\mathbb F_{13}\), \(n=3,4\).
    The last four were computed after the law was stated, as a test of it.
    • Two trials deviate, both in the small fields \(\mathbb F_{121}\) and \(\mathbb F_{169}\). At \(\mathbb F_{13}\), \(n=3\), one of two trials has \(\kappa(11)=4\), \(\kappa(12)=3\), with the orders 11 and 12 swapped.
    • Reruns: \(\mathbb F_{13}\), \(n=3\) with six trials matches the law in all six. \(\mathbb F_{11}\), \(n=3\) with six trials matches in five. \(\mathbb F_{11^3}\), \(n=3\) with six trials matches in all six.
    • A specialization can move orders, not only lose them. So a deviating trial is a special point, provided the generic answer is what most points give. That proviso is itself an assumption. Every trial is reported in initial-segment-all-trials.txt.
  • Tests without it. In both computed cases without the hypothesis, \(\mathbb F_5\) with \(n=4,5\) (\(C_3=5\)), the law fails, as the lemma requires.
  • Caveat. All \(\kappa\) are computed at random specializations, not generically, so the evidence is statistical.

Consequences if true. In characteristic 0, on the real cube studied by Sauermann and Wigderson, the stable per-order values would be

\[ \delta=n+2(k-1)-\min\{d:N_{2,n}(d)>k-\ell-1\}\qquad\text{for large }\ell, \]

a closed form in terms of the generating function \((1+t)^n/(1-t^2)\). The law is the unique nondecreasing \(\kappa\) with the count \(N_{2,n}\), and it minimizes \(\max_{m'\le m}\kappa(m')\) for every \(m\). Individual values can lie below it without the hypothesis: over \(\mathbb F_5\), \(n=4\), \(\kappa(5)=1\).

General statement. conj:cube-initial-segment-law, for every field with \(C_0,\ldots,C_{n-1}\ne0\), all \(n\) and \(d\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the linear-form lemma, the capped Catalan bound, the stable law with its count, and the square theorem. Claim searches (initial segment, Catalan, cube orders) found this thread's related results only. A fresh-context referee checked the lemma in both directions, the equivalence of the two forms of the conjecture, the \(n\le2\) cases, the \(n=3\) exception list, and reran the checker. It found that one \(\mathbb F_{13}\) trial contradicts the law, which the first checker hid by reading only one trial. The checker now reports every trial, the reruns above were made, and the evidence is stated per trial. Its other changes are made: \(\kappa_3(0)\), the \(n=1\) argument, the "as efficient as possible" and assessment wording, and the existence of \(n\) allowed parts.

Assessment. The exceptions to the capped count have a conjectural explanation: orders fill initial segments, reached by nonlinear polynomials in \(z\) that products of linear forms miss. A proof needs to show \(m\in\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)\) for every \(m<N_{2,n}(d)\); the count then gives equality. The natural attempt is induction on \(d\), multiplying \(\mathrm{Ord}(d-1)\) by \(\mathrm{Ord}(1)\), using the exact kernel from the sharpness lemma to show that no new order is lost. The next step tries this. The first new case is \(n=3\), \(d=2\), order 7. Products of two linear forms give the orders 4, 5 and 6, but not 7.

Individually measured conversion, checks, and local processing0.20 s

25 September 2026 — The cube's order determinant: exact proofs for small degree, and a refined initial-segment law

Working proof of the determinant criterion, with finite checks for n ≤ 5. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The initial-segment law was supported by random specializations. Can it be proved, at least case by case, and what exactly does its hypothesis control?

The determinant criterion

Working lemma (lem:cube-order-determinant). Fix \(n,d\) and put \(N=N_{2,n}(d)\). Let \(\Delta_{n,d}\in\mathbb Z[y_1,\ldots,y_n]\) be the determinant of the \((N-1)\times(N-1)\) matrix of the coefficients of \(T^1,\ldots,T^{N-1}\) in the elements \(T^Qz^r\) with \(r\in\{0,1\}^n\), \(2Q+|r|\le d\) and \((Q,r)\ne(0,0)\). Here \(z_i=\sum_j(-1)^jC_{j-1}(y_iT)^j\).

  1. Over a field \(F\), \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)=\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\) if and only if \(\Delta_{n,d}\ne0\) in \(F[y]\). Over \(\mathbb F_p\) this means \(p\nmid\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{n,d})\).
  2. If some specialization \(y^*\in\overline{\mathbb F}_p^{\,n}\) with \(y^*_1\ne0\) has specialized orders \(\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\), then \(\Delta_{n,d}\not\equiv0\pmod p\). Then the law at \((n,d)\) holds over every field of characteristic \(p\), and over every field of characteristic 0.

Proof.

  • Part 1. By the sharpness lemma, in the form extended to every finite grid in the finite-grid stable law (it uses only \(G_i\) and \(\deg(L-X^2)<2\)), the \(T^Qz^r\) (including \(1\)) form a basis of the span of the monomials of degree \(\le d\), which has dimension \(N\). An element with nonzero constant term has order 0. So \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)=\{0\}\cup\mathrm{Ord}(V')\), where \(V'\) is the span of the non-constant basis elements, all of positive order. \(\mathrm{Ord}(V')\) has \(N-1\) elements. It equals \(\{1,\ldots,N-1\}\) if and only if truncation to \(T^1,\ldots,T^{N-1}\) is injective on \(V'\), that is, if and only if the square matrix is nonsingular.
  • Part 2. The reduction behind the sharpness lemma writes each monomial of degree \(\le d\) as a combination of the \(T^Qz^r\) with coefficients in \(\mathbb Z[y,y_1^{-1}]\). So at \(y^*\) with \(y^*_1\ne0\), the span of the specialized monomials lies inside the span of the \(N\) specialized basis elements. If the specialized orders are \(\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\), that span has dimension \(N\) and those orders, so the specialized square matrix is nonsingular and \(\Delta_{n,d}(y^*)\ne0\).
    • Hence \(\Delta_{n,d}\not\equiv0\) modulo \(p\), and \(\Delta_{n,d}\ne0\) in \(\mathbb Z[y]\).
    • By part 1 the law holds over \(\mathbb F_p(y)\), over \(F(y)\) for any \(F\supseteq\mathbb F_p\), and over \(\mathbb Q(y)\).
    • For fields of characteristic 0: \(\Delta\ne0\) over \(\mathbb Z\) stays nonzero over any field containing \(\mathbb Q\). \(\square\)

For \(d=1\), \(\Delta_{n,1}=\prod_{j=1}^n(-1)^jC_{j-1}\cdot\prod_iy_i\cdot\prod_{i<i'}(y_{i'}-y_i)\). Its content is \(\prod_{j<n}C_j\), which is the degree-one lemma again.

Exact values

Finite check (check:cube-initial-segment-certificates). research/tools/bmd_cube_order_determinant.py builds the matrix, and Singular computes \(\Delta_{n,d}\), its integer content and its factorization:

  • \(\Delta_{3,2}=-4\,(y_1y_2y_3)^4\prod_{i<j}(y_i-y_j)^4(y_1-y_2-y_3)(y_1-y_2+y_3)(y_1+y_2-y_3)\), content \(4\);
  • \(\Delta_{3,3}\): content \(2\), four irreducible quadratics, the \(y_i\) and \(y_i-y_j\) to the power 9;
  • \(\Delta_{3,4}\): content \(8\), the three factors \(y_1\pm y_2\pm y_3\), three irreducible quartics, the \(y_i\) and \(y_i-y_j\) to the power 16;
  • \(\Delta_{4,2}\): content \(8\), one irreducible factor of degree 15 and content 1, the \(y_i\) and \(y_i-y_j\) to the power 5.

The attempt at \(\Delta_{5,2}\) (\(16\times16\) in 5 variables) ended without output, most likely stopped by the memory limit.

So the law holds for \(n=3\), \(d\le4\), and for \(n=4\), \(d=2\), in every odd characteristic and in characteristic 0. The two deviating trials of the previous entry are zeros of these determinants. The \(\mathbb F_{121}\) trial lies on \(y_1+y_2=y_3\) and deviates at \(d=2,4\). The \(\mathbb F_{169}\) trial lies on none of the linear factors and deviates only at \(d=3\), so it is a zero of a quadratic factor of \(\Delta_{3,3}\).

By part 2, every random trial that reproduced the initial segments is itself a certificate for the degrees it verified. From the recorded trials (refined-law.txt), certified cases in characteristic 0 include \(n=4\), \(d\le3\) and \(n=5\), \(d\le2\). With \(n=2\) (square theorem) and \(n=1\), the stable per-order law on the cube in characteristic 0 is therefore proved in these cases:

  • \(n=3\): every \(m\le15\);
  • \(n=4\): every \(m\le N_{2,4}(3)-1=19\);
  • \(n=5\): every \(m\le N_{2,5}(2)-1=16\).

In these cases \(\kappa_n(m)=\min\{d:N_{2,n}(d)>m\}\).

The refined law

The contents found for \(d\ge2\) are powers of 2. They carry no trace of the Catalan hypothesis. And over \(\mathbb F_5\), \(n=4\), where \(C_3=5\) spoils degree one, the orders at degree 2 are again \(\{0,\ldots,11\}\). This suggests that the hypothesis only matters at \(d=1\).

Conjecture (conj:cube-refined-initial-segment-law). Over every field of characteristic \(\ne2\):

  • \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(1)=\{0\}\cup\{n\text{-allowed parts}\}\), which is proved;
  • \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)=\{0,\ldots,N_{2,n}(d)-1\}\) for every \(d\ge2\).

Equivalently, \(\kappa_n(m)=1\) for \(n\)-allowed \(m\), and \(\kappa_n(m)=\max(2,\min\{d:N_{2,n}(d)>m\})\) for the other \(m\ge1\). This implies the earlier conjecture. In characteristic 2 that conjecture's hypothesis fails for \(n\ge3\), since \(C_2=2\), and \(n\le2\) is proved. It also explains the \(\mathbb F_5\) values with \(n=4,5\) that contradicted the earlier conjecture: \(\kappa_4(4)=2\) and \(\kappa_4(5)=1\) at \(n=4\); \(\kappa_5(4)=2\) and \(\kappa_5(6)=1\) at \(n=5\). The refined law matches at least half of the trials in all 15 computed \((n,p)\) cases. In the one case with a single match out of two, \(\mathbb F_{169}\) with \(n=3\), the six-trial rerun matches in all six. It matches every trial in the larger fields \(\mathbb F_{243}\), \(\mathbb F_{125}\), \(\mathbb F_{343}\) and \(\mathbb F_{11^3}\) (refined-law.txt). Equivalently, the conjecture says that \(\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{n,d})\) is a power of 2 for every \(n\) and \(d\ge2\).

General statement. conj:cube-refined-initial-segment-law, for every field of characteristic \(\ne2\), all \(n\) and \(d\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the sharpness lemma and its finite-grid extension, the degree-one lemma and the previous entry's trial data. Claim searches (determinant, initial segment, cube orders) found only this thread's related results. A fresh-context referee checked both parts of the lemma (including the direction of the span inclusion under specialization), the \(d=1\) formula, reran \(\Delta_{3,2}\), checked the saved determinants and the certified ranges, and found no mathematical gap. Its corrections are made: the explanation of the \(\mathbb F_{169}\) deviating trial, which is a zero of a quadratic factor and not of a linear one; the "majority" claim; the sharpness citation; the Part 2 wording; the regenerated \(\Delta_{3,2}\) file; and the status line.

Assessment. The law has become a statement about one family of integer determinants, whose computed contents are small powers of 2. The degree-one factor \(\prod C_j\) is the only Catalan arithmetic that appears. A proof needs a structural reason for the factorization. Candidates are the multiplicities \(d^2\) of the Vandermonde factors at \(n=3\), and the factors \(y_1\pm y_2\pm y_3\), which suggest a product formula over sign vectors. Finding and proving that formula is the next step.
