25 September 2026 — The cube's order determinant as a Hermite–Padé normality problem, and certificates for every odd prime up to 67

Working proof of the Hermite–Padé form, with finite checks for odd p ≤ 67. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The refined law says that \(\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{n,d})\) is a power of 2 for \(d\ge2\) (determinant criterion). Does any odd prime divide a content in a larger range of \((n,d)\)? And which known problem is this, so that the right methods can be brought in? The test ends with either an odd prime \(p\) and a case where every trial fails, or certificates in every case.

The Hermite–Padé form

Working lemma (lem:cube-hermite-pade-form). Let \(F\) be a field of characteristic \(\ne2\), let \(a_i=4y_i\) and \(w_i=1-2z_i\in F[y][[T]]\). Then \(w_i^2=1+a_iT\) and \(w_i\) has constant term 1, so \(w_i=(1+a_iT)^{1/2}\). For \(r\in\{0,1\}^n\) put \(w^r=\prod_{i:r_i=1}(1+a_iT)^{1/2}\) and \(D_r=\lfloor(d-|r|)/2\rfloor\) (for \(|r|\le d\)). Then:

  1. The span of the polynomials of degree \(\le d\) in \(z_1,\ldots,z_n\) over \(F(y)\) is \(\bigoplus_{|r|\le d}w^r\cdot F(y)[T]_{\le D_r}\), and \(\sum_r(D_r+1)=N_{2,n}(d)\).
  2. \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)=\{0,\ldots,N_{2,n}(d)-1\}\) over \(F\) if and only if no nonzero family \((p_r)\) with \(\deg p_r\le D_r\) gives \(\sum_rp_rw^r\in T^{N_{2,n}(d)}F(y)[[T]]\). In the language of Hermite–Padé approximation, the multi-index \((D_r)_r\) is normal for the type I problem of the system \(\{w^r:|r|\le d\}\).
  3. Let \(\Delta^w_{n,d}\) be the \(N\times N\) determinant of the coefficients of \(T^0,\ldots,T^{N-1}\) in the \(T^Qw^r\) (\(2Q+|r|\le d\)). Then \(\Delta^w_{n,d}=\pm2^{e}\Delta_{n,d}\) with \(e=\sum_{2Q+|r|\le d}|r|\).

Proof. \((1-2z_i)^2=1+4(z_i^2-z_i)=1+4y_iT\), since \(z_i^2-z_i=y_iT\). A power series with constant term 1 over a field of characteristic \(\ne2\) has exactly one square root with constant term 1, so \(w_i=(1+a_iT)^{1/2}\).

  • Part 1. Expand \(w^r=\prod_{r_i=1}(1-2z_i)=\sum_{r'\le r}(-2)^{|r'|}z^{r'}\). So for fixed \(Q\), the change from the \(T^Qz^{r}\) to the \(T^Qw^{r}\) (\(2Q+|r|\le d\)) is triangular for the order \(r'\le r\). The set of allowed \(r\) is closed downwards, and the diagonal entries \((-2)^{|r|}\) are units. By the sharpness lemma, in the form extended to every finite grid in the finite-grid stable law (as in Part 1 of the criterion), the \(T^Qz^r\) are a basis of the span of the monomials of degree \(\le d\). Hence so are the \(T^Qw^r\), which gives the direct sum and the count \(\sum_r(D_r+1)=\#\{(Q,r):2Q+|r|\le d\}=N_{2,n}(d)\).
  • Part 2. The span has dimension \(N=N_{2,n}(d)\), and elements with distinct orders are independent, so it has at most \(N\) distinct orders. Elimination on lowest orders gives a basis with \(N\) distinct orders, so there are exactly \(N\). These are \(\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\) if and only if no nonzero element has order \(\ge N\).
  • Part 3. In the \(z\) basis the column of \(1\) is the first unit vector and every other column has zero constant term, so the full \(N\times N\) determinant of the \(T^Qz^r\) equals \(\Delta_{n,d}\). The triangular change of Part 1 multiplies it by \(\prod_{2Q+|r|\le d}(-2)^{|r|}=(-2)^e\). \(\square\)

So the refined law in characteristic \(p\ne2\) says: the multi-index \((\lfloor(d-|r|)/2\rfloor)_r\) is normal for the system of the products of square roots \(\prod_{i\in r}(1+a_iT)^{1/2}\), \(|r|\le d\), with generic centres, over \(\mathbb F_p(a)\). Characteristic 2 is exactly where \(w_i\) is unavailable, and the degree-one Catalan factors are the normality defects of the index with \(D_r=0\) for \(|r|\le1\). For \(n=1\) this is the Padé table of \((1+aT)^{1/2}\). A classical example of a perfect system in characteristic 0, one in which every multi-index is normal, is the binomial system \((1-z)^{\omega_1},\ldots,(1-z)^{\omega_m}\) with \(\omega_i-\omega_j\notin\mathbb Z\), all centred at one point. Ours has one exponent and several centres, and uses products. It is not among the standard perfect systems, and one literature search found no result covering it. A proof therefore has to be built. Normality in characteristic 0 alone gives only \(\Delta_{n,d}\ne0\) in \(\mathbb Z[y]\); the refined law's real content is the statement for each odd \(p\).

Certificates for every odd prime up to 67

Finite check (check:cube-determinant-certificates-many-primes). research/tools/bmd_cube_det_certify.py evaluates the \((N-1)\times(N-1)\) matrix of \(\Delta_{n,d}\) at random points \(y^*\in\mathbb F_{p^a}^{\,n}\), \(p^a\ge300\) (sampled with nonzero coordinates). It reports the first trial (of at most 4) with a nonzero determinant. The script reduces the integer matrix defining \(\Delta_{n,d}\) mod \(p\), so \(\Delta_{n,d}(y^*)\ne0\) in \(\mathbb F_{p^a}\) proves \(\Delta_{n,d}\not\equiv0\pmod p\) directly. Part 1 of the criterion then gives the law over every field of characteristic \(p\), and \(\Delta_{n,d}\ne0\) in \(\mathbb Z[y]\) gives characteristic 0.

  • Cases. The 32 pairs with \(2\le n\le6\), \(2\le d\le12\) and \(N_{2,n}(d)\le50\): \(n=2,3\) with \(d\le12\), \(n=4\) with \(d\le6\), \(n=5\) with \(d\le4\), and \(n=6\) with \(d\le3\).
  • Odd primes. Each of the 18 odd primes \(3\le p\le67\) certifies all 32 cases (576 certificates). 571 of them come from the first trial and five from the second. The run then reached the 120 s limit while building the tables for \(p=71\), so the file ends at \(p=67\).
  • Control. \(p=2\), where the contents found so far are even, certifies the 11 cases with \(n=2\), where the square law holds over every field. It fails all 4 trials in each of the 21 cases with \(n\ge3\), as it must if \(2\mid\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{n,d})\) there.

So in each of the 32 cases, no prime \(3\le p\le67\) divides \(\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{n,d})\). By the criterion the refined law holds there over every field of characteristic \(3\le p\le67\) and of characteristic 0. With the degree-one lemma for \(d=1\), this determines \(\kappa_n(m)\) as the refined law predicts in these characteristics:

  • \(n=3\): every \(m\le N_{2,3}(12)-1=47\);
  • \(n=4\): every \(m\le N_{2,4}(6)-1=43\);
  • \(n=5\): every \(m\le N_{2,5}(4)-1=47\);
  • \(n=6\): every \(m\le N_{2,6}(3)-1=48\).

The outputs are in research/results/bmd-r28-determinant-formula/certify.txt. The earlier arithmetic, which recomputed base-\(p\) digits for each field operation, timed out after the \(p=2\) control. The script now uses addition and multiplication tables, and was checked to reproduce the earlier run's output on a smaller grid.

A failed shortcut: one extremal coefficient

If one coefficient of \(\Delta_{n,d}\) were \(\pm2^k\), the content would divide it and the refined law would follow at \((n,d)\). The lex-extremal coefficients (Singular, lex-leading.txt) are:

  • \(\pm1\) for \(n=2\), \(d=2,3,4\);
  • \(-4\) for \((3,2)\), with content 4;
  • \(6\) for \((3,3)\), with content 2;
  • \(-8008=-2^3\cdot7\cdot11\cdot13\) for \((4,2)\), with content 8.

So the lex-extremal coefficient decides \((3,2)\) but not \((3,3)\) or \((4,2)\). The natural way to compute an extremal coefficient in general is by degeneration, one variable at a time, and it meets a collision. Sending \(y_n\to0\) replaces \(z_n\) by \(-y_nT\), which turns the space into \(V_{n-1,d}+T\,V_{n-1,d-1}\). That sum is not direct for \(d\ge2\), since both terms contain \(T\,V_{n-1,d-2}\). So the determinant of the limit matrix vanishes, the lowest power of \(y_n\) in \(\Delta_{n,d}\) exceeds the number of columns with \(r_n=1\), and its coefficient is set by higher-order terms of \(z_n\), which bring in Catalan numbers. A degeneration argument would need these collisions resolved, which is a confluent Hermite–Padé problem.

General statement. lem:cube-hermite-pade-form, for every field of characteristic \(\ne2\) and all \(n,d\). The certificates test conj:cube-refined-initial-segment-law.

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the determinant criterion, the sharpness lemma and its finite-grid extension, and the degree-one lemma. Claim searches (Hermite–Padé, perfect system, normal, square root) found no recorded result; one web search found the classical single-centre binomial perfect systems only. The certifier reproduced its earlier run on a smaller grid, with a \(p=2\) negative control. A fresh-context referee checked all three parts of the lemma (triangularity, count, exponent and sign), every count in certify.txt, the \(\kappa\) ranges, the script's matrix, the extremal coefficients and the degeneration argument, and found no mathematical gap. Its corrections are made: certification by direct evaluation of \(\Delta_{n,d}\) rather than by specialized orders, the exact case range, the citation of the extended sharpness lemma, the second half of Part 2, the restriction to \(|r|\le d\), the characteristic-0 scope of perfectness and of the analytic tools, and the range comparison.

Assessment. The falsification test found no odd prime in any content over a range about two to three times as long in \(m\) as before, now in every characteristic \(3\le p\le67\); the refined law is not refuted in these cases. The Hermite–Padé form turns the law into a normality statement about an explicit system of algebraic functions, where the natural tools are explicit remainders (contour integrals or hypergeometric closed forms) and the orthogonality duality between type I and type II. These give nonvanishing in characteristic 0 only; to control the content mod \(p\) they have to produce an integral closed form for \(\Delta^w_{n,d}\) or its content. The lowest nontrivial degree \(d=2\) is the natural first target for all \(n\). There the span is \(1\), the \(w_i\), the \(w_iw_j\) and \(T\), with \(N=(n^2+n+4)/2\).

25 September 2026 — The cube's orders on a curve: the initial-segment law in characteristic 0 for all n, and in dimension 3 in every odd characteristic

Working proofs: the refined law in characteristic 0, and for n = 3 in characteristic ≠ 2. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The refined law says that the degree-\(\le d\) orders fill \(\{0,\ldots,N_{2,n}(d)-1\}\) for \(d\ge2\) in characteristic \(\ne2\). Its Hermite–Padé form involves the functions \(w_i=(1+a_iT)^{1/2}\) with \(a_i=4y_i\). These live on the curve \(C_a:\ w_i^2=1+a_iT\) (\(i=1,\ldots,n\)). Can the law be proved from the geometry of that curve? The test ends with a proof or with a concrete obstruction on \(C_a\).

The curve and the transport lemma

Let \(F\) be an algebraically closed field of characteristic \(\ne2\), and let \(a\in F^n\) have distinct nonzero entries. Let \(C_a\subset\mathbb A^{n+1}\) be the affine curve \(w_i^2=1+a_iT\), and let \(V_d\) be the space of functions on \(C_a\) given by polynomials of degree \(\le d\) in \(w_1,\ldots,w_n\). Put \(N=N_{2,n}(d)\) and \(p_0=(w,T)=(1,\ldots,1,0)\).

Working lemma (lem:cube-curve-transport).

  1. \(C_a\) is irreducible, and the \(N\) functions \(T^Qw^r\) (\(r\in\{0,1\}^n\), \(2Q+|r|\le d\)) are a basis of \(V_d\).
  2. Let \(q\in C_a\) be a point with \(b=T(q)\) and \(1+a_ib\ne0\) for all \(i\). Put \(\lambda_i=w_i(q)\) and \(a'_i=a_i/\lambda_i^2\), which are distinct and nonzero. Expand the functions of \(V_d\) in the local parameter \(s=T-b\) at \(q\). The resulting orders are the orders of the Hermite–Padé problem at \(a'\), that is, the orders \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)\) at the specialization \(y^*=a'/4\).
  3. Consequently, \(\Delta_{n,d}\not\equiv0\) in characteristic \(\mathrm{char}\,F\) if and only if some such \(C_a\) has a point \(q\) as in 2 at which the orders of \(V_d\) are \(0,1,\ldots,N-1\).

Proof.

  • Part 1. The polynomials \(1+a_iT\) are distinct irreducibles in \(F[T]\). So their classes in \(F(T)^*/F(T)^{*2}\) are independent, and by Kummer theory \(F(T)(w_1,\ldots,w_n)\) has degree \(2^n\) over \(F(T)\) with basis \(\{w^r\}\). Hence \(C_a\) is irreducible, and \(\sum_rp_r(T)w^r=0\) forces every \(p_r=0\), so the \(T^Qw^r\) are independent. They lie in \(V_d\), since \(T=(w_1^2-1)/a_1\). Conversely, a monomial of degree \(e\le d\) in the \(w_i\) reduces by \(w_i^2=1+a_iT\) to a combination of \(T^Qw^r\) with \(2Q+|r|\le e\).
  • Part 2. Since \(1+a_ib\ne0\), each \(\lambda_i\ne0\), and \(T\) is unramified at \(q\), so \(s\) is a local parameter there. Put \(w'_i=w_i/\lambda_i\). Then \(w'^2_i=(1+a_ib+a_is)/\lambda_i^2=1+a'_is\), because \(\lambda_i^2=1+a_ib\), and \(w'_i(q)=1\). So in \(F[[s]]\), \(w'_i\) is the square root \((1+a'_is)^{1/2}\) with constant term 1. Scaling the variables preserves the polynomials of degree \(\le d\), so \(V_d\) is the span of the polynomials of degree \(\le d\) in the \(w'_i\). By the Hermite–Padé form (whose Part 1 holds for these \(a'\) by Part 1 here), this is the span of the \(s^Qw'^r\) at \(a'\). The \(a'_i\) are distinct because \(a'_i=a'_j\) would give \(a_i(1+a_jb)=a_j(1+a_ib)\), that is \(a_i=a_j\).
  • Part 3. By the Hermite–Padé form (Part 3), \(\Delta^w_{n,d}=\pm2^e\Delta_{n,d}\) as polynomials. \(\Delta^w_{n,d}(a')\) is the determinant of the Taylor coefficients of orders \(0,\ldots,N-1\) of the \(N\) basis functions. It is nonzero exactly when their orders are \(0,\ldots,N-1\). If \(\Delta_{n,d}\not\equiv0\), choose \(a\) with distinct nonzero entries and \(\Delta_{n,d}(a/4)\ne0\), and take \(q=p_0\). Conversely, a point \(q\) as stated gives \(\Delta_{n,d}(a'/4)\ne0\). \(\square\)

So the law at \((n,d)\) is the statement that the linear series \(V_d\) on the curves \(C_a\) has the standard orders \(0,\ldots,N-1\) at some point, that is, at a general point. In the language of Stöhr and Voloch, \(V_d\) is classical. The special point \(p_0\) has become a general one.

Characteristic 0: every \(n\) and \(d\)

Theorem (thm:cube-initial-segment-char-zero). \(\Delta_{n,d}\ne0\) in \(\mathbb Z[y]\) for all \(n,d\ge1\). Hence over every field of characteristic 0:

  • \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)=\{0,\ldots,N_{2,n}(d)-1\}\) for every \(d\);
  • \(\kappa_n(m)=\min\{d:N_{2,n}(d)>m\}\) for every \(m\);
  • by the stable per-order law, the savings on the real cube satisfy \(h(n,k,\ell)\le\kappa_n(k-\ell-1)\), with equality once \(\ell\) is large for fixed \(m=k-\ell-1\).

Proof. Take \(F=\overline{\mathbb Q}\) and any \(a\) with distinct nonzero entries, and let \(f_1,\ldots,f_N\) be the basis of Part 1.

  • The derivatives \(d^if_j/dT^i\) are rational functions on \(C_a\), regular wherever all \(w_i\ne0\), since \(dw_i/dT=a_i/(2w_i)\). So the Wronskian \(W=\det(d^if_j/dT^i)_{0\le i<N,\,1\le j\le N}\) is a rational function on \(C_a\).
  • At \(p_0\), where \(T\) is a local parameter, the expansions of the \(f_j\) are linearly independent power series over \(F\) (Part 1). In characteristic 0 the Wronskian of independent power series is nonzero. So \(W\ne0\), and \(W\) vanishes at only finitely many points of the irreducible curve \(C_a\).
  • Choose \(q\) with \(W(q)\ne0\) and \(1+a_iT(q)\ne0\) for all \(i\). The Taylor coefficient matrix \((f_j^{(i)}(q)/i!)\) has determinant \(W(q)/\prod_{i<N}i!\ne0\), so the orders of \(V_d\) at \(q\) are \(0,\ldots,N-1\).
  • By the transport lemma, \(\Delta_{n,d}\ne0\) in characteristic 0. The first bullet of the theorem is then the determinant criterion. The second follows because then \(m\in\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)\) if and only if \(N_{2,n}(d)>m\). The third is the stable per-order law, whose \(\kappa\) is the least degree containing \(m\). \(\square\)

This settles in characteristic 0 both the initial-segment conjecture and the refined law. There the Catalan hypothesis \(C_0,\ldots,C_{n-1}\ne0\) always holds. In characteristic \(p\) the argument breaks down: the ordinary Wronskian vanishes identically once \(N>p\), and even its Hasse-derivative version can vanish identically, for a non-classical series. Ruling that out for \(V_d\) is the open step.

Dimension 3 in every odd characteristic

Theorem (thm:cube-dimension-three-law).

  1. Torsion criterion. Let \(F\) be algebraically closed of characteristic \(\ne2\) and \(a\in F^3\) with distinct nonzero entries. Let \(\bar C_a\) be the smooth projective model of \(C_a\), an elliptic curve with origin \(p_0\), and let \(c_1\) be the point \((w,T)=(-1,1,1,0)\). The orders at \(y^*=a/4\) of the polynomials of degree \(\le d\) are \(\{0,\ldots,4d-1\}\) if and only if \(d\cdot2c_1\ne0\).
  2. The law. Over every field of characteristic \(\ne2\), \(\mathrm{Ord}_3(d)=\{0,\ldots,4d-1\}\) for every \(d\ge1\). Hence \(\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{3,d})\) is a power of 2 for every \(d\), and \(\kappa_3(m)=\lfloor m/4\rfloor+1\) for \(m\ge1\) over every field of characteristic \(\ne2\).

Proof of the criterion.

  1. The elliptic curve. The map \(\bar C_a\to\mathbb P^1_T\) has degree 8, with group \(G=\{\pm1\}^3\) acting by sign changes. Over each \(T=-1/a_i\) the inertia group is generated by the flip \(\sigma_i\) of \(w_i\). Over \(T=\infty\) it is generated by the flip of all three \(w_i\), since each \(w_i\sim(a_iT)^{1/2}\). So there are 4 places over each of these 4 points, each with ramification index 2. Riemann–Hurwitz (tame, since the characteristic is \(\ne2\)) gives \(2g-2=8\cdot(-2)+4\cdot4\), so \(g=1\). The four places at infinity form a divisor \(H_\infty\) of degree 4, and each \(w_i\) has a simple pole at each of them. So \(V_d\subseteq L(dH_\infty)\). Both have dimension \(4d\): \(V_d\) by Part 1 of the transport lemma, and \(L(dH_\infty)\) by Riemann–Roch. So \(V_d=L(dH_\infty)\), and \(N_{2,3}(d)=4d\).
  2. Reduction to a divisor class. A nonzero \(f\in V_d\) with order \(\ge4d\) at \(p_0\) exists if and only if \(L(dH_\infty-4d\,p_0)\ne0\). Since the degree is 0, this holds if and only if \(dH_\infty\sim4d\,p_0\). By the Hermite–Padé form, the orders at \(p_0\) in the parameter \(T\) are the orders at \(y^*=a/4\).
    • The sign changes. Each \(\sigma_i\) is an involution with fixed points, namely the places over \(T=-1/a_i\). Taking one of them as origin, \(\sigma_i\) is an automorphism of order 2 of the elliptic curve fixing the origin. In every characteristic the group of such automorphisms has \([-1]\) as its only element of order 2. It is cyclic of order 2, 4 or 6, or of order 12 in characteristic 3 with \(j=0\), or of order 24 in characteristic 2 (Silverman, The Arithmetic of Elliptic Curves, III.10.1). So \(\sigma_i(x)=-x+c_i\) with \(c_i=\sigma_i(p_0)\), the point over \(T=0\) with \(w_i=-1\) and the other coordinates 1. Then \(\sigma_i\sigma_j\) is the translation by \(c_i-c_j\), and it is an involution, so \(2c_i=2c_j\).
    • The hyperplane class. The function \(w_1-1\) has polar divisor \(H_\infty\). Its zeros are the four points over \(T=0\) with \(w_1=1\), each simple: \(p_0\), \(c_2\), \(c_3\) and \(\sigma_2\sigma_3(p_0)=c_2-c_3\). Their sum in the group is \(2c_2\).
    Hence \(H_\infty-4p_0\) corresponds to the point \(2c_1\), and the orders are \(\{0,\ldots,4d-1\}\) if and only if \(d\cdot2c_1\ne0\). \(\square\)

Proof of the law. Let \(k\) be the prime field and \(K=k(a_1,a_2,a_3)\) with independent transcendentals \(a_i\). By the determinant criterion the law over fields of characteristic \(\mathrm{char}\,k\) is \(\Delta_{3,d}(a/4)\ne0\) at this generic \(a\). By the Hermite–Padé form (Part 3) this says that the specialized orders at \(a\in\bar K^3\) are \(\{0,\ldots,4d-1\}\). By the criterion with \(F=\bar K\), it remains to show that \(c_1\) has infinite order. The points \(p_0\) and \(c_1\) are \(K\)-rational, so this can be decided over \(K\).

  1. Isogeny. Let \(E':Y^2=(1+a_1T)(1+a_2T)(1+a_3T)\), with origin at infinity. It is smooth because the cubic has distinct roots and nonzero leading coefficient. Let \(P_0=(0,1)\). The map \(\psi:(w,T)\mapsto(T,w_1w_2w_3)\) identifies \(E'\) with the quotient of \(\bar C_a\) by the even sign changes \(\{1,\sigma_i\sigma_j\}\): the fixed field of that subgroup is \(K(T,w_1w_2w_3)\). The map \(\varphi(x)=\psi(x)-P_0\) sends \(p_0\) to the origin, so it is an isogeny. Its fibres are the orbits of the translations by \(c_i-c_j\), so \(\ker\varphi=\{0,c_1-c_2,c_1-c_3,c_2-c_3\}\cong(\mathbb Z/2)^2\). It sends \(c_1\mapsto(0,-1)-P_0=-2P_0\), so if \(c_1\) is torsion then so is \(P_0\).
  2. \(P_0\) has infinite order. Suppose \(mP_0=O\) over \(K\) for some \(m\ge1\).
    • Spreading out. Let \(U\subset\mathrm{Spec}\,k[a]\) be the open set where \(a_1a_2a_3\ne0\) and the \(a_i\) are distinct. Over \(U\), \(E'\) is an elliptic curve (in the monic model \(X=a_1a_2a_3T\)). The sections \(mP_0\) and \(O\) agree on a closed subset that contains the generic point, hence on all of \(U\), and so after every base change \(\mathrm{Spec}\,L\to U\).
    • A base change with a non-torsion point. Let \(E_0:v^2=u(u-1)(u+1)\) over \(k\), which is smooth since \(p\ne2\). Let \(L=k(E_0)\) with generic point \((u_0,v_0)\), and put \(a_i=1/(u_0-e_i)\) for \(e=(0,1,-1)\). These are distinct and nonzero. Then \(u=u_0+T\), \(v=v_0Y\) is an isomorphism \(E'_L\to E_0\otimes L\): indeed \(v^2=v_0^2\prod(1+T/(u_0-e_i))=\prod(u-e_i)\). It fixes the points at infinity and sends \(P_0\) to \((u_0,v_0)\). Under the correspondence between \(E_0(L)\) and rational maps \(E_0\dashrightarrow E_0\), this point is the identity map, and \(m(u_0,v_0)\) is the map \([m]\), which is not constant. So \(m(u_0,v_0)\ne O\), a contradiction.

So \(c_1\) has infinite order, \(d\cdot2c_1\ne0\) for every \(d\ge1\), and the law holds at every \(d\). Since \(\mathrm{Ord}_3(d)=\{0,\ldots,N(d)-1\}\), \(m\in\mathrm{Ord}_3(d)\) if and only if \(N_{2,3}(d)=4d>m\), which gives \(\kappa_3\). \(\square\)

The factors of \(\Delta_{3,d}\). By the criterion, on \(U\) the determinant \(\Delta_{3,d}(a/4)\) vanishes exactly where the order of \(2c_1\) divides \(d\). That order is never 1, since \(\Delta_{3,1}\) is \(\prod_iy_i\prod_{i<j}(y_j-y_i)\) times the constant \(\pm C_0C_1C_2=\pm2\), which is nonzero in characteristic \(\ne2\). So \(\Delta_{3,2}\) vanishes on \(U\) exactly on the order-2 locus, \(\Delta_{3,3}\) exactly on the order-3 locus, and \(\Delta_{3,4}\) on the orders 2 and 4.

Matching these loci with the recorded factorizations is an identification that relies on those factorizations. The linear factors \(y_1\pm y_2\pm y_3\), common to \(\Delta_{3,2}\) and \(\Delta_{3,4}\), give order 2. The quadratic factors of \(\Delta_{3,3}\) give order 3. The quartics of \(\Delta_{3,4}\) give order 4.

The deviating specializations of the earlier trials are consistent with this. The \(\mathbb F_{121}\) trial lies on \(y_1+y_2=y_3\) and deviates at \(d=2,4\). The \(\mathbb F_{169}\) trial deviates at \(d=3\).

Check of the torsion criterion

Finite check (check:cube-elliptic-orders). By the torsion criterion (with \(F=\overline{\mathbb F}_p\)), at a specialization \(a\) the first failing degree \(d_{\min}\) is the order of \(2c_1\). Since \(\varphi(2c_1)=-4P_0\) and \(\ker\varphi\) has exponent 2, that order is \(o'\) or \(2o'\), where \(o'=\mathrm{ord}(4P_0)\). research/tools/bmd_cube_elliptic_check.py takes random \(a\in\mathbb F_p^3\) with distinct nonzero entries (\(p=5,\ldots,23\), 8 each). It computes \(\mathrm{ord}(P_0)\) on \(E'(\mathbb F_p)\) by repeated addition and \(d_{\min}\le12\) by evaluating \(\Delta_{3,d}(a/4)\). All 56 trials agree (54 distinct specializations, since two repeat; research/results/bmd-r29-degree-two/elliptic-n3.txt):

  • 14 with \(o'=1\), \(d_{\min}=2\);
  • 8 with \(o'=2\), \(d_{\min}=4\);
  • 18 with \(o'=3\), \(d_{\min}=3\), and 6 with \(o'=3\), \(d_{\min}=6\);
  • 7 with \(o'=5\), \(d_{\min}=5\);
  • 3 with \(o'=7\), \(d_{\min}=7\).

Both alternatives \(o'\) and \(2o'\) occur. Every \(d_{\min}\) found is at most 7, so the bound \(d_{\min}\le12\) was never reached.

General statement. thm:cube-initial-segment-char-zero, for all \(n,d\) in characteristic 0. thm:cube-dimension-three-law covers every \(d\) at \(n=3\) in every characteristic \(\ne2\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the Hermite–Padé form, the determinant criterion, the stable per-order law and the degree-one formula for \(\Delta_{n,1}\). Claim searches (Wronskian, Weierstrass, classical linear series, torsion, elliptic, initial segment in characteristic 0) found only this thread's results; lem:root-curve-independence is the characteristic-2 independence lemma, not a version of these. The automorphism fact is cited from Silverman III.10.1 as a standard textbook statement, not re-read here. The torsion criterion was tested numerically at 54 specializations (check:cube-elliptic-orders). A fresh-context referee checked the transport lemma, the Wronskian theorem and every step of the elliptic theorem, including characteristic 3 with \(j=0\), and found no error that breaks a theorem. Its corrections are made: the torsion criterion is stated for every specialization and the generic case is reduced to it over \(\bar K\); the involution fact is justified; the kernel of the isogeny is proved; the determinant factors are derived from the criterion, and the earlier trials are marked as consistency observations; duplicate trials and the unreached bound are noted; the Stöhr–Voloch route is stated as unverified. The referee also proposed recording the large-\(p\) consequence of Stöhr–Voloch as a lemma now. That waits until the source has been read.

Assessment. In characteristic 0 the per-order question on the cube is now settled for large \(\ell\): the savings are \(\kappa_n(m)=\min\{d:N_{2,n}(d)>m\}\), with \(m=k-\ell-1\). In odd characteristic the refined law is now the statement that the linear series \(V_d\) on the diagonal complete intersections \(C_a\) is classical. Dimension 3 is proved by the elliptic argument. For \(n\ge4\) the curve has genus \(1+2^{n-2}(n-3)\), and \(V_d\) has degree \(2^{n-1}d\). Two routes are open:

  • Stöhr and Voloch's \(p\)-adic criterion, as usually quoted, says that if \(\varepsilon\) is an order at a general point and \(\binom{\varepsilon}{\mu}\not\equiv0\pmod p\), then \(\mu\) is also one. Every order of \(V_d\) is at most its degree \(2^{n-1}d\), so for \(p>2^{n-1}d\) it would give classicality, and with the transport lemma \(p\nmid\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{n,d})\). This has not been checked against the source; reading the exact statement and hypotheses is the next step. What would remain open is \(3\le p\le2^{n-1}d\).
  • A generalization of the elliptic argument, in which the classes \(dH_\infty-N\,p_0\) avoid the theta divisor for all large \(d\).

25 September 2026 — The cube's initial-segment law for p above the degree, and every odd characteristic for n ≤ 6 at small degree

Working proof for p > 2^{n-1}d; with certificates, the law for n ≤ 6 at small degree in every odd characteristic. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The transport lemma turned the refined law in characteristic \(p\) into classicality of the series \(V_d\) on the curves \(C_a:w_i^2=1+a_iT\). The previous entry left the Stöhr–Voloch route unchecked against its source. Does their order criterion apply, and which primes does it cover? The test ends with the exact statement read and either a proof or a missing hypothesis.

Source. K.-O. Stöhr and J. F. Voloch, Weierstrass points and curves over finite fields, Proc. London Math. Soc. (3) 52 (1986) 1–19, §1, read through the exposition by F. Torres (arXiv math/0011091, §2): Hasse derivatives (§2.1), Wronskians, Lemma 2.7 and Corollary 2.10. The original paper was not accessible. The lemma below is proved in full here, following Torres's proof of Lemma 2.7, so it does not rest on the citation.

The binomial order criterion

Let \(F\) be algebraically closed of characteristic \(p>2\), let \(a\in F^n\) have distinct nonzero entries, and let \(V_d\) and \(N=N_{2,n}(d)\) be as in the transport lemma. For a point \(P\) of \(C_a\) with \(1+a_iT(P)\ne0\) for all \(i\) (so \(t=T-T(P)\) is a local parameter), let \(j_0<\cdots<j_{N-1}\) be the orders at \(P\) of the functions in \(V_d\).

Working lemma (lem:cube-binomial-order-criterion). If the integer

\[ \det\Bigl(\tbinom{j_\ell}{i}\Bigr)_{0\le\ell,i<N}=\frac{\prod_{\ell<\ell'}(j_{\ell'}-j_\ell)}{\prod_{i<N}i!} \]

is not divisible by \(p\), then \(p\nmid\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{n,d})\).

Proof.

  • The determinant. \(\binom xi=x^i/i!+(\text{lower terms})\), so column operations turn the matrix into the Vandermonde matrix \((j_\ell^i)\) divided by \(\prod i!\). It is an integer matrix, so the determinant is an integer.
  • Hasse derivatives. The field \(F(C_a)\) is separable of degree \(2^n\) over \(F(T)\), since the characteristic is odd. So the Hasse derivatives \(D^i=D_T^i\) of \(F(T)\) extend uniquely to \(F(C_a)\), satisfying \(D^0=\mathrm{id}\), \(D^i|_F=0\) for \(i\ge1\), the product rule, and \(D^i\circ D^j=\binom{i+j}iD^{i+j}\) (Torres §2.1).
    • At a point \(Q\) unramified over \(T\), expansion in \(s=T-T(Q)\) is an embedding \(F(C_a)\hookrightarrow F((s))\). The Hasse derivatives \(D_s^i\sum c_ks^k=\sum\binom kic_ks^{k-i}\) on \(F((s))\) have the same properties and agree with \(D_T^i\) on \(F(T)\). They map the image of \(F(C_a)\) into itself: applying \(D_s^i\) to the minimal polynomial relation of a generator over \(F(T)\) determines \(D_s^i\) of the generator recursively, because the derivative of the minimal polynomial does not vanish there (Torres (2.1), (2.2)). So they agree with the extended \(D_T^i\), and the embedding commutes with them.
    • So \((D^if)(Q)\) is the coefficient of \(s^i\) in the expansion of \(f\) at \(Q\).
  • The Wronskian. Since \(1+a_iT(P)\ne0\), \(P\) is unramified over \(T\), so the previous bullet applies at \(P\). Let \(g_0,\ldots,g_{N-1}\) be a basis of \(V_d\) with \(\mathrm{ord}_Pg_\ell=j_\ell\), and write \(g_\ell=\sum_{k\ge j_\ell}c^\ell_kt^k\) at \(P\) with \(c^\ell_{j_\ell}\ne0\). Let \(W=\det(D^ig_\ell)_{i,\ell<N}\in F(C_a)\). At \(P\), \(D^ig_\ell=\sum_k\binom kic^\ell_kt^{k-i}\). Every term of the expanded determinant has \(t\)-order at least \(\sum_\ell j_\ell-\sum_ii\). The coefficient of \(t^{\sum j_\ell-\sum i}\) is \(\prod_\ell c^\ell_{j_\ell}\cdot\det\binom{j_\ell}i\), which is nonzero by hypothesis. So \(W\ne0\).
  • A point with standard orders. \(W\) is a nonzero rational function on the irreducible curve \(C_a\), so it has finitely many zeros and poles. Choose \(Q\) with \(W(Q)\ne0\), \(1+a_iT(Q)\ne0\) for all \(i\), and every \(D^ig_\ell\) regular at \(Q\). By the second bullet, \(W(Q)\) is the determinant of the Taylor coefficients of orders \(0,\ldots,N-1\) of the basis at \(Q\). So the orders at \(Q\) are \(0,\ldots,N-1\), and the transport lemma (Part 3) gives \(\Delta_{n,d}\not\equiv0\pmod p\). \(\square\)

Primes above the degree

Theorem (thm:cube-large-characteristic). For all \(n,d\ge1\) and every odd prime \(p>2^{n-1}d\), \(p\nmid\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{n,d})\). So over every field of odd characteristic \(p>2^{n-1}d\), \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)=\{0,\ldots,N_{2,n}(d)-1\}\). In particular every odd prime factor of \(\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{n,d})\) is at most \(2^{n-1}d\), and for fixed \((n,d)\) the refined law is a finite check.

Proof. Take \(P=p_0\) on any \(C_a\) (\(a\) with distinct nonzero entries).

  • Pole count. Over \(T=\infty\) the inertia group of the sign-change group \(\{\pm1\}^n\) is generated by the flip of all the \(w_i\), since each \(w_i\sim(a_iT)^{1/2}\). So there are \(2^{n-1}\) places, each with ramification index 2. At each of them \(T\) has a pole of order 2, and since \(w_i^2=1+a_iT\), each \(w_i\) has a simple pole. The \(w_i\) are integral over \(F[T]\), so they are regular at every place over a finite value of \(T\). A polynomial of degree \(\le d\) in the \(w_i\) therefore has poles only at infinity, of total order \(\le2^{n-1}d\).
  • Orders are small. Every nonzero \(f\in V_d\) therefore has at most \(2^{n-1}d\) zeros, counted with multiplicity, so \(j_{N-1}\le2^{n-1}d<p\).
  • The binomial condition. The differences \(j_{\ell'}-j_\ell\) all lie in \([1,p-1]\), so the numerator is prime to \(p\), and so is the integer of the lemma, and the lemma applies. The second sentence is the determinant criterion. \(\square\)

The degree bound is crude: at \(p_0\) over a generic \(a\) the orders are expected to be \(0,\ldots,N-1\), far below \(2^{n-1}d\). Any point with small or \(p\)-adically spread orders would lower the threshold.

Every odd characteristic for n ≤ 6 at small degree

Extended certificates. This run extends the range of check:cube-determinant-certificates-many-primes rather than registering a new check. The finisher limits new finite checks on this route, and here the certificates only delimit the theorem's consequences. The theorem leaves, for each \((n,d)\), the odd primes \(p\le2^{n-1}d\). The certificates of 25 September cover \(3\le p\le67\) for \(N_{2,n}(d)\le50\). One further run of research/tools/bmd_cube_det_certify.py covers \(2\le n\le6\), \(2\le d\le3\) and the 23 odd primes \(3\le p\le89\), with 4 trials each (research/results/bmd-r30-large-p/certify-n6d3.txt). All 230 cases are certified. These include \((6,3)\) for the primes \(71\le p\le89\) below \(2^5\cdot3=96\). Together:

  • \(n=4\), \(2\le d\le6\) (\(2^3d\le48\));
  • \(n=5\), \(2\le d\le4\) (\(2^4d\le64\));
  • \(n=6\), \(2\le d\le3\) (\(2^5\cdot3=96\), with every odd prime below 96 certified).

In these cases \(\mathrm{content}(\Delta_{n,d})\) is a power of 2, so the refined law holds over every field of characteristic \(\ne2\). At \(d=1\) the content is \(\pm C_0\cdots C_{n-1}\), which contains \(C_3=5\), \(C_4=14\) and \(C_5=42\). There the degree-one lemma gives \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(1)=\{0\}\cup\{n\text{-allowed parts}\}\) instead of an initial segment. With it, \(\kappa_n(m)\) is determined by the refined formula (\(1\) for \(n\)-allowed \(m\), otherwise \(\max(2,\min\{d:N_{2,n}(d)>m\})\)) in every characteristic \(\ne2\) for \(m\le43\) at \(n=4\), \(m\le47\) at \(n=5\) and \(m\le48\) at \(n=6\). Together with the dimension-3 theorem and the square theorem, the stable per-order law on the cube is now determined in every odd characteristic for \(n\le6\) at these orders.

General statement. thm:cube-large-characteristic, for all \(n,d\) and all odd \(p>2^{n-1}d\).

Verification gate. Source read: Torres (arXiv math/0011091) §2.1 on Hasse derivatives, Lemma 2.7 and Corollary 2.10; the original Stöhr–Voloch paper was not accessible, and the lemma is proved in full here. Dependencies re-read: the transport lemma and the determinant criterion. Claim searches (Stöhr–Voloch, Wronskian, classical, large characteristic) found no recorded version. A fresh-context referee compared the lemma with Torres's Lemma 2.7 and found no omitted hypothesis. It also checked the determinant identity, the leading term of the Wronskian, the place count at infinity, the order bound, and the coverage of every odd prime \(p\le2^{n-1}d\) in both certificate files, including the 230 count and the \(\kappa\) ranges. No theorem broke. Its corrections are made: the restriction to odd \(p\); \(d\ge2\) in the certified cases, with \(d=1\) left to the degree-one lemma; the case count; the Hasse-derivative agreement at a point; unramifiedness at \(P\); regularity at finite places; and an unneeded clause.

Assessment. The refined law is now proved in characteristic 0, for \(n\le3\) in every odd characteristic, and for \(p>2^{n-1}d\) in general. For each fixed case this leaves finitely many small primes. The gap is the range \(3\le p\le2^{n-1}d\), which grows with \(d\). Two ways to close it:

  • A point of \(C_a\) with explicitly known orders whose binomial determinant is prime to every odd \(p\). Candidates are the ramification points \(w_i=0\) and the places at infinity.
  • A generalization of the elliptic argument.

25 September 2026 — Route review of the cube: the large-ℓ savings are known, and the recorded values lag them by at most one

Route review; the proved κ passes a test against all 1109 recorded values over F_3, F_5 and F_7. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

The line's general claim. The line aims to determine \(\delta_F(n,k,\ell)\) on the cube \(\{0,1\}^n\) over fields of characteristic \(\ne2\), equivalently the savings \(h=n+2(k-1)-\delta\), with \(m=k-\ell-1\). Five research entries since the last review have changed the picture:

  • The large-\(\ell\) limit \(\kappa_n(m)\) of the savings (stable per-order law) is now proved to be \(\min\{d:N_{2,n}(d)>m\}\) in characteristic 0 for every \(n\) (theorem).
  • In odd characteristic \(p\) it is given by the refined formula (1 on the \(n\)-allowed parts, otherwise \(\max(2,\min\{d:N_{2,n}(d)>m\})\)). This is proved for \(n\le3\) (elliptic argument), for \(p>2^{n-1}d\) (theorem), and for \(n\le6\) at small \(m\) (certificates).
  • The mechanism is geometric. The degree-\(d\) root-curve polynomials form a linear series on the curves \(w_i^2=1+a_iT\), and the law says that this series is classical (transport lemma).

What the main goal needs. Nothing. The thread's goal, \(D(n,k)\) over \(\mathbb F_2\), is proved and formalized. The line gives the preprint its context: it reproves and extends Menezes' result, and it now also gives the stable per-order values on the real cube.

Falsification attempt: the recorded values against the proved κ

The exact per-order values recorded over \(\mathbb F_3\) and \(\mathbb F_5\) (\(n\le5\), \(k\le13\), research/results/bmd-r9-odd-cube) and over \(\mathbb F_7\) (\(n\le4\), \(k\le11\), research/results/bmd-r25-cube-review) were computed before any of the new results. In this range \(m\le12\), so the refined formula for \(\kappa_n(m)\) is proved in all of it: for \(n\le3\) by the elliptic argument, and for \(n=4,5\) by the certificates, since \(12\le43\).

The stable law says \(h\le\kappa_n(m)\) at every \(\ell\). So a single recorded value with \(h>\kappa\) would refute the new theorems or the old data. The new mode --kappa of research/tools/bmd_cube_rho_cap_check.py compares them (research/results/bmd-r31-route-review/kappa-p3.txt, kappa-p5.txt, kappa-p7.txt):

  • No violation. No value exceeds \(\kappa\). Of the 1109 values (455, 390 and 264), 1089 equal \(\kappa\): 450, 378 and 261.
  • The 20 values below \(\kappa\) all equal \(\kappa-1\), and all occur at small \(\ell\).
    • At \((n,m)=(3,8)\), in all three characteristics, \(h=2<3\) for \(\ell=0,1,2\). Over \(\mathbb F_3\) and \(\mathbb F_5\), \(h=\kappa\) from \(\ell=3\) on, as far as recorded (\(\ell\le4\) and \(\ell\le3\)). Over \(\mathbb F_7\) only \(\ell\le2\) is recorded.
    • At \((3,12)\) over \(\mathbb F_3\), and \((4,12)\) over \(\mathbb F_3\), only \(\ell=0\) is recorded, and it is below \(\kappa\).
    • Over \(\mathbb F_5\): \((4,6),(4,7),(4,8)\) at \(\ell=0\); \((5,7),(5,8),(5,10)\) at \(\ell\le1\).

The values \(m=8=N_{2,3}(2)\) and \(m=12=N_{2,3}(3)=N_{2,4}(2)\) are the first orders that need a new degree. The \(\mathbb F_5\) cases sit where \(C_3=5\) removes the part 4 at degree one. So the lag appears where \(\kappa\) jumps.

Assessment and next step

This line has moved from a sandwich to a determination of the large-\(\ell\) savings:

  • Characteristic 0: complete for every \(n\).
  • Odd characteristic: complete for \(n\le3\), and for \(p>2^{n-1}d\) in general. What is left is the finitely many small primes of each case.

What remains open for \(\delta_F\) itself is the pre-stable regime, the values of \(h\) at small \(\ell\) before they reach \(\kappa\). Of the two open items it is the higher risk: it is the only one without a mechanism, while the small-prime question has a method and certificates. The data make it look narrow and regular. \(h\) is either \(\kappa\) or \(\kappa-1\), and the deficit appears only at small \(\ell\) where \(\kappa\) jumps.

The next research step, on this item, is to state and attack a one-step lag law: \(\kappa_n(m)-1\le h\le\kappa_n(m)\) for every \(\ell\), with an exact description of when \(h=\kappa\). The candidate mechanism is the origin-order constraint. Order exactly \(\ell\) at the origin forces the jet's lowest part, which costs at most one degree against the root-curve construction. The first test is the lower bound \(h\ge\kappa-1\), from a construction that loses one degree, against the 20 lagging values.

25 September 2026 — Pre-stable savings on the cube: the one-step lag fails, and thresholds are initial degrees of ideals

Refutation of the one-step lag law, with a working proof of the threshold-ideal lemma. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. The route review found every recorded pre-stable value within one of \(\kappa_n(m)\), and proposed a one-step lag law, \(\kappa-1\le h\le\kappa\) at every \(\ell\). Is it true beyond \(k\le13\)? What exactly governs the \(\ell\) at which the savings reach a given value? The test ends with a value \(h<\kappa-1\), or with agreement over a range where the law's predictions differ from other candidate laws.

The one-step lag law fails

Refutation (ex:cube-one-step-lag-fails). research/tools/bmd_cube_lag_check.py runs the compiled cube kernel bmd_jet_orders_q --cube for \(k=1,\ldots,K\). Each \(k\) gives \(\delta(n,k,\ell)\) for every \(\ell<k\) in one elimination. The script compares \(h\) with the proved \(\kappa_3(m)=\lfloor m/4\rfloor+1\) (dimension-3 theorem). It first reproduced the recorded \(\mathbb F_3\) values for \(k\le13\). The runs:

  • \(n=3\) over \(\mathbb F_{101}\), \(k\le30\): 465 values (research/results/bmd-r32-one-step-lag/lag-p101-n3.txt).
  • \(n=3\) over \(\mathbb F_3\): \(k\le32\), 528 values, before the 120 s limit stopped \(k=33\) (lag-p3-n3.txt).

At \(m=28\), \(\kappa_3(28)=8\), but \(h=6\): over \(\mathbb F_{101}\) at \(\ell=0,1\), and over \(\mathbb F_3\) at \(\ell=0,1,2\). So the lag can be 2. No value exceeds \(\kappa\), and the two tables agree at all 465 common points.

The table over \(\mathbb F_{101}\) shows the structure.

  • Order zero. At \(\ell=0\), \(h=\lfloor(m+1)/5\rfloor+1\) for \(1\le m\le29\). This has period 5, against period 4 for \(\kappa_3\), so the lag at \(\ell=0\) grows like \(m/20\).
  • Thresholds. The least \(\ell\) with savings \(\ge h\):
    • 0 at \(m=5h-6\) (for \(2\le h\le7\));
    • 3 at \(m=5h-7\) (for \(3\le h\le6\));
    • at the \(\kappa\)-boundary \(m=4(h-1)\): 3, 8, 15 for \(h=3,4,5\). The value 15 is from \(\mathbb F_3\) (\(k=32\)); over \(\mathbb F_{101}\) it is only known to exceed 13.

Thresholds are initial degrees

Work on the cube over a field \(F\), with \(R=F[y_1,\ldots,y_n]\), the root curve \(z_i\), and \(X_{a,r}\) the degree-\(r\) part of \(\prod_ir_0(y_i)^{a_i}\), as in the stable per-order law and its finite-grid form. For \(h\ge1\) and \(m\ge0\), let \(\mathrm{Sol}^{(h)}_m\subseteq R^{m+1}\) be the set of \((W_0,\ldots,W_m)\) with

\[ \sum_{c\le m}W_cX_{a,c-t}=0\qquad(a\in\{0,1\}^n,\ |a|+2t\le h-1), \]

and let \(I^{(h)}_m=\{W_m:W\in\mathrm{Sol}^{(h)}_m\}\).

Working lemma (lem:cube-threshold-ideal).

  1. \(I^{(h)}_m\) is a homogeneous ideal of \(R\), decreasing in \(h\). For \(k=\ell+m+1\), the savings satisfy \(h(k,\ell)\ge h\) if and only if \(I^{(h)}_m\) has a nonzero element of degree \(\ell\), that is, \(\ell\ge\ell_0(h,m):=\mathrm{indeg}\,I^{(h)}_m\). Hence \(h(k,\ell)=\max\{h:\ell_0(h,m)\le\ell\}\), and \(\kappa(m)=\max\{h:I^{(h)}_m\ne0\}\), with \(\mathrm{indeg}\,0=\infty\) and \(\max\emptyset=0\). For example, \(I^{(1)}_0=0\), since the equation for \(a=0\), \(t=0\) is \(W_0=0\).
  2. The first order outside \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(h-1)\). Suppose \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(h-1)=\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\) with \(N=N_{2,n}(h-1)\), and take \(m=N\). Here \(\kappa(N)\ge h\), with equality when \(N\in\mathrm{Ord}_n(h)\); in that case we call \(m=N\) the \(\kappa\)-boundary. Then \(\mathrm{Sol}^{(h)}_m\) is free of rank 1, and \(\ell_0(h,m)=\deg\Delta_{n,h-1}-\deg g\), where \(g\) is the gcd of the \(N\times N\) minors of the constraint matrix.

Proof.

  • Part 1.
    • By the condition system in the proof of the stable per-order law, in the form of the finite-grid law ("Lemma (a) and the converse in the stable law's part (3) then go through verbatim", with the Lagrange basis and triangularity step), a jet \(v\) of order exactly \(\ell\) has savings \(\ge h\) if and only if the homogeneous parts \(\tilde w^{(c)}\) of degree \(\ell+m-c\) of \(\tilde w=v(r_0(y))/\prod_iL'(r_0(y_i))\) satisfy these equations with \(\tilde w^{(m)}\ne0\). The derivation of the condition system there never uses the value \(h=\kappa(m)\), so it holds for every \(h\). Since \(L'(0)=-1\) is a unit, \(v\mapsto\tilde w\) is an order-preserving bijection on truncated series, so every such family with \(\tilde w^{(m)}\ne0\) comes from a jet of order exactly \(\ell\).
    • The equations are \(R\)-linear, so \(\mathrm{Sol}\) is an \(R\)-module and \(I\) is an ideal. They are also homogeneous: each \(X_{a,r}\) is homogeneous of degree \(r\), so the components \(W_c^{[D-c]}\) of a solution again form a solution for every \(D\). Hence \(\mathrm{Sol}\) is graded (with \(W_c\) in degree \(D-c\)), and \(I\) is homogeneous.
    • So savings \(\ge h\) at \((k,\ell)\) holds exactly when \(I\) has a nonzero element of degree \(\ell\). Since \(I\) is an ideal, this then holds for every larger degree.
    • More constraints give smaller \(I\), so \(I\) decreases in \(h\). The formula for \(h(k,\ell)\) follows. For \(\kappa\), \(I^{(h)}_m\ne0\) exactly when some \(\ell\) attains \(h\), and the stable law identifies the largest such \(h\) with \(\kappa(m)\).
  • Part 2.
    • The constraints are indexed by the \(N\) elements \(T^tz^a\) with \(|a|+2t\le h-1\). The constraint of \(f\) is \(\sum_cW_c[T^c]f=0\), so the constraint matrix is the \(N\times(m+1)\) matrix of coefficients of \(T^0,\ldots,T^N\).
    • Its minor omitting the column \(c=m\) is the determinant of the coefficients of \(T^0,\ldots,T^{N-1}\). The row of the constraint \(f=1\) is the first unit vector, and expanding along it gives \(\pm\Delta_{n,h-1}\), which is nonzero by the hypothesis and the determinant criterion. So over \(K=F(y)\) the matrix has rank \(N\), and its kernel is the line spanned by the vector \(M\) of signed maximal minors (Cramer).
    • \(R\) is a unique factorization domain, so \(\mathrm{Sol}=K M\cap R^{m+1}=R\cdot(M/g)\), with \(M/g\) primitive. The minors are homogeneous, so \(I=R\cdot(M_m/g)\) with \(M_m=\pm\Delta_{n,h-1}\), and its initial degree is \(\deg\Delta_{n,h-1}-\deg g\). \(\square\)

Observation. In dimension 3 and characteristic \(\ne2\), the hypothesis of Part 2 holds for every \(h\), with equality \(\kappa(4(h-1))=h\) (dimension-3 theorem). The boundary thresholds 3, 8, 15 equal the degrees of the factors of \(\Delta_{3,h-1}\) other than the \(y_i\) and the \(y_i-y_j\). By the recorded factorizations, these are:

  • \(\Delta_{3,2}\): three linear forms \(y_1\pm y_2\pm y_3\);
  • \(\Delta_{3,3}\): four quadratics;
  • \(\Delta_{3,4}\): three linear forms and three quartics.

These are the loci where the point \(2c_1\) of the dimension-3 theorem has order dividing \(h-1\), with degrees 3, 8, 15, that is \((h-1)^2-1\). By Part 2, this match says exactly that \(g\) is the part of \(\Delta_{3,h-1}\) made of the \(y_i\) and \(y_i-y_j\), which is the conjecture below, not something the data establish. The factorizations are over \(\mathbb Z\), while the value 15 comes from \(\mathbb F_3\); the recorded contents 4, 2, 8 only rule out \(\Delta\) vanishing mod 3, so the match at \(h=5\) assumes the factorization pattern survives mod 3. The threshold 15 was predicted before the \(\mathbb F_3\) value was read.

Conjecture (conj:cube-dimension-three-thresholds). On \(\{0,1\}^3\) over every field of characteristic \(\ne2\):

  1. Let \(g_{3,d}\) be the lemma's \(g\) at \(n=3\), \(h=d+1\). Then \(g_{3,d}\) is, up to a constant, \((y_1y_2y_3)^{d^2}\prod_{i<j}(y_i-y_j)^{d^2}\), of degree \(6d^2\). Hence the savings reach \(\kappa=d+1\) at \(m=4d\) exactly from \(\ell=d^2-1\) on, the degree of the \(d\)-division locus of \(2c_1\). The "hence" is Part 2 with \(N=4d\) and \(\deg\Delta_{3,d}=\sum_{c<4d}c-\sum Q=(8d^2-2d)-(d^2-2d+1)\), that is \(7d^2-1\). Here \(\sum Q\) runs over the basis \((Q,r)\), and this degree formula was checked for \(d\le29\). So \(\ell_0=7d^2-1-6d^2=d^2-1\).
  2. At origin order 0, \(h(3,k,0)=\lfloor k/5\rfloor+1\) for \(k\ge2\), that is, \(\delta(3,k,0)=2k-\lfloor k/5\rfloor\).

Part 1 matches \(d=1,2,3,4\) (thresholds 0, 3, 8, 15). Part 2 matches all \(k\le30\) over \(\mathbb F_{101}\) and all \(k\le32\) over \(\mathbb F_3\). Over \(\mathbb F_2\) the binary theorem gives \(\delta(3,k,0)=3+\sum_{j<3}\lfloor(k-1)/2^j\rfloor\), asymptotically about \(7k/4<9k/5\) (at \(k=5\) it is 10 against 9), so the formula is specific to characteristic \(\ne2\).

General statement. lem:cube-threshold-ideal, for the cube over every field, all \(n\), \(m\) and \(h\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the condition system of the stable per-order law and its finite-grid form, the determinant criterion with the recorded factorizations, and the dimension-3 theorem. Claim searches (order zero, threshold, pre-stable, cube dimension three) found only the \(\mathbb F_2\) order-zero theorem, which the entry compares with. The lag checker reproduced the recorded \(\mathbb F_3\) values for \(k\le13\) before the new runs. A fresh-context referee re-parsed both result files and confirmed every data claim. It confirmed both parts of the lemma, including the homogeneity of the minors, and checked \(\deg\Delta_{3,d}=7d^2-1\) for \(d\le29\). Its corrections are made: the condition system on \(\tilde w\) over every field; its validity for every \(h\); the conventions for empty ideals; the row, not column, of \(f=1\); the name and scope of the boundary case and of the dimension-3 hypothesis; the degree computation behind the conjecture; the observation label, with the mod-3 caveat; the asymptotic binary comparison; and the status line.

Assessment. The pre-stable regime is not a bounded lag. It is governed by the initial degrees of the ideals \(I^{(h)}_m\). At the \(\kappa\)-boundary these are the degrees of the primitive part of the order determinant, which in dimension 3 is a division-polynomial locus. So the full per-order function on the cube is now a question about these ideals.

  • At the boundary, the next step is to prove the gcd statement for \(n=3\): the \(y_i\) and \(y_i-y_j\) divide every maximal minor to the power \(d^2\), and the torsion factors divide none.
  • Away from the boundary, \(\mathrm{Sol}\) has higher rank and \(I\) is generated by several minors. The order-zero formula is the other end of the same family.

25 September 2026 — The boundary thresholds in dimension 3: only collision factors divide the gcd, and the exponent is d² on test lines up to d = 10

Working proof that only collision factors divide the boundary gcd (n = 3), with finite checks for d ≤ 10. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. By the threshold-ideal lemma, the savings on \(\{0,1\}^3\) reach \(d+1\) at \(m=4d\) exactly from \(\ell_0=\deg\Delta_{3,d}-\deg g\). Here \(g\) is the gcd of the maximal minors of the boundary constraint matrix. The conjecture says that \(g\) is the collision part \((y_1y_2y_3)^{d^2}\prod_{i<j}(y_i-y_j)^{d^2}\) of \(\Delta_{3,d}\), so \(\ell_0=d^2-1\). Which half can be proved, and does the prediction hold beyond \(d=4\)? The test ends with a \(d\) where \(g\) contains another factor or a different exponent, or with agreement at the predicted thresholds 24, 35, … beyond the observed 0, 3, 8, 15.

Only collision factors divide the gcd

Proposition (prop:cube-boundary-torsion-coprime). Let \(F\) have characteristic \(\ne2\) and \(d\ge1\). Let \(A\) be the \(4d\times(4d+1)\) matrix of the coefficients of \(T^0,\ldots,T^{4d}\) in the \(T^Qz^r\) (\(r\in\{0,1\}^3\), \(2Q+|r|\le d\)), and \(g\) the gcd of its maximal minors in \(F[y_1,y_2,y_3]\). At every \(y^*\in\overline F^{\,3}\) with distinct nonzero entries, \(A(y^*)\) has rank \(4d\). Hence the maximal minors have no common zero off the hyperplanes \(y_i=0\) and \(y_i=y_j\), every prime factor of \(g\) is one of the \(y_i\) or \(y_i-y_j\), and \(g\) divides the collision part of \(\Delta_{3,d}\). So the threshold \(\ell_0(d+1,4d)\) is at least the degree of the part of \(\Delta_{3,d}\) prime to \(\prod_iy_i\prod_{i<j}(y_i-y_j)\).

Proof.

  • The curve. Put \(a^*=4y^*\). By the dimension-3 theorem (its paragraph "The elliptic curve"), \(\bar C_{a^*}\) is a smooth curve of genus 1, and \(V_d=L(dH_\infty)\) has dimension \(4d\).
  • A basis. Since \(z^r=\prod_{r_i=1}(1-w_i)/2\) is \((-1/2)^{|r|}w^r\) plus terms \(w^{r'}\) with \(r'<r\), the \(T^Qz^r\) are triangular in the basis \(T^Qw^r\) of the transport lemma (Part 1). So they form a basis of \(V_d\).
  • The matrix. The rows of \(A(y^*)\) are the \(T\)-expansions at \(p_0\) of these \(4d\) functions: each \(z_i=(1-w_i)/2\) is a function on the curve whose expansion is the specialized Catalan series.
  • Full rank. \(A(y^*)\) has rank \(4d\) if and only if no nonzero element of \(V_d\) vanishes to order \(\ge4d+1\) at \(p_0\), that is, if and only if \(L(dH_\infty-(4d+1)p_0)=0\). This holds because the divisor has degree \(-1\).
  • Consequences. The gcd of the minors does not change under field extension, so we may compute over \(\overline F\). A prime factor of \(g\) other than a collision factor would vanish at some point with distinct nonzero entries (Nullstellensatz), where all minors would vanish, which is impossible. Since \(g\) divides \(M_N=\pm\Delta_{3,d}\), it divides the collision part. By the threshold-ideal lemma (Part 2, which applies since \(\mathrm{Ord}_3(d)\) is an initial segment in characteristic \(\ne2\)), \(\ell_0=\deg\Delta_{3,d}-\deg g\), which gives the bound. \(\square\)

What remains for the conjecture is the exponent: every maximal minor must be divisible by \(y_i^{v}\) and \((y_i-y_j)^{v}\), where \(v=v_{y_i}(\Delta_{3,d})\), and that exponent must equal \(d^2\). This is a statement about the degenerations \(a_i\to0\) and \(a_i\to a_j\), where two branch points of the curve collide and the elliptic curve acquires a node. The exponent \(d^2\) has the quadratic growth of a local height, which suggests computing it from the reduction of \(2c_1\) at those degenerations.

The gcd along a line, up to d = 10

Finite check (check:cube-boundary-gcd-degrees).

  • Kernel. The compiled kernel research/tools/bmd_cube_boundary_minors.cpp restricts \(y\) to an affine line \(y=u+tv\) with random \(u,v\) over \(\mathbb F_{1000003}\). It evaluates every maximal minor \(M_c(t)\) at more points than any of their degrees, interpolates, and takes \(\gcd_c M_c(t)\) over \(\mathbb F_p\). It then reports the multiplicity of each root where the line meets \(y_i=0\) or \(y_i=y_j\), and the degree left over, for \(g\) and for \(M_N=\pm\Delta_{n,d}\).
  • Driver. research/tools/bmd_cube_boundary_gcd.py runs it for a range of \(d\).
  • What a line shows. Restriction can only enlarge the gcd: \(g|_{\text{line}}\) divides \(\gcd_cM_c(t)\). So the computed gcd gives, rigorously for \(\mathbb F_{1000003}\), an upper bound on \(\deg g\) and on each collision multiplicity, and hence a lower bound on \(\ell_0\). This needs two conditions, both met on the recorded lines. First, \(g(v)\ne0\) for the direction \(v\): this holds because \(\deg_tM_N\) on the line equals the homogeneous degree \(\sum_{c<N}c-\sum Q\) of \(\Delta\), so \(\Delta(v)\ne0\) and hence \(g(v)\ne0\). Second, the collision roots are pairwise distinct, which the driver asserts. Equality holds unless the line meets the codimension-2 common zero set of the \(M_c/g\), which a random line avoids with high probability. The equalities below are therefore finite-check evidence, and the inequalities are certificates.

Results (research/results/bmd-r33-boundary-gcd/gcd-n3.txt, gcd-n4.txt):

  • \(n=3\), \(d=1,\ldots,10\):
    • \(\deg\Delta_{3,d}=7d^2-1\);
    • on the line, \(g\) and \(\Delta_{3,d}\) both have multiplicity \(d^2\) at each of the six collision roots, and \(g\) has nothing else. This certifies \(\deg g\le6d^2\) and \(v(\Delta)\le d^2\), and is evidence for equality;
    • certified \(\ell_0\ge d^2-1\) over \(\mathbb F_{1000003}\) (0, 3, 8, 15, 24, 35, 48, 63, 80, 99), with equality on the line as evidence.
    The first four match the kernel thresholds of the previous entry, which came from \(\mathbb F_{101}\) and, for 15, \(\mathbb F_3\).
  • Kernel test at \(d=5\). The per-order kernel at \(n=3\), \(k=44\) over \(\mathbb F_{101}\) (770 s, p101-n3-k44.json) gives \(h=5\) at \(m=20\), \(\ell=23\). So the savings have not yet reached 6 at \(\ell=23\). This confirms \(\ell_0\ge24\) independently of the minors, the half already certified. The run at \(k=45\) (1078 s, p101-n3-k45.json) gives \(h=6\) at \(m=20\), \(\ell=24\). So over \(\mathbb F_{101}\) the threshold is exactly \(\ell_0(6,20)=24=5^2-1\), found by exact per-order computation independent of the minors. This is the first observed threshold beyond 15.
  • \(n=4\), \(d=1,\ldots,5\):
    • \(g\) is again exactly a power of the ten collision factors, with exponent \(1,5,13,25,41=d^2+(d-1)^2\), the same as in \(\Delta_{4,d}\);
    • \(\ell_0=0,15,55,115,195\), which is \(10d^2-10d-5\) for \(d\ge2\);
    • \(\deg\Delta_{4,d}=10,65,185,365,605\).

On the tested lines the gcd is exactly the collision part of \(\Delta_{n,d}\) in both dimensions, with exponents \(d^2\) for \(n=3\) and \(d^2+(d-1)^2\) for \(n=4\). For \(n=4\) nothing is proved.

General statement. prop:cube-boundary-torsion-coprime, for every \(d\ge1\) in characteristic \(\ne2\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the threshold-ideal lemma (Part 2), the dimension-3 theorem's elliptic-curve paragraph, and the transport lemma's basis. Claim searches (boundary, gcd, threshold, collision) found only this thread's results. A fresh-context referee checked the proposition and found it correct. It noted that the proof never uses that the factor divides \(\Delta\) or is a torsion factor, and the statement is now in that stronger form. It also checked the kernel code (interpolation nodes, the degree bound, gcd and multiplicities), reran \(d=2\) over two fields, and confirmed every listed number. Its corrections are made: the stronger statement; the basis step; the direction and point of the specialization; the extension-of-scalars step; the citation; the conditions under which the line computation certifies (\(\Delta(v)\ne0\), distinct collision roots, now asserted in the driver); certified inequalities kept apart from evidence for equality; the \(n=4\) summary; and the fields behind the earlier thresholds. It also pointed out that the \(k=44\) run tests only the half already certified; the \(k=45\) run now tests the other half, and it agrees.

Assessment. Half of the boundary conjecture is proved for all \(d\): only collision factors can divide the gcd, so the threshold is at least the degree of the rest of \(\Delta_{3,d}\). The other half, that the collision factors divide every minor to the exact power, is a local computation at the nodal degenerations. The computations support it through \(d=10\), and they show the same shape for \(n=4\) with exponent \(d^2+(d-1)^2\). The next step is that local computation, which would prove \(\ell_0=d^2-1\) for \(n=3\).

25 September 2026 — The boundary thresholds in dimension 3 are d² − 1: the collision exponent is d²

Working proof of the dimension-3 boundary thresholds, with a collision bound in every dimension. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. By the previous proposition, only the collision factors \(y_i\) and \(y_i-y_j\) divide the gcd \(g\) of the boundary minors on \(\{0,1\}^3\). The conjecture \(\ell_0(d+1,4d)=d^2-1\) therefore reduces to the exponent: \(v_D(g)=d^2\) for each collision divisor \(D\). Can the exponent be computed from the degeneration at \(D\)? The test ends with a proof or with a divisor whose exponent differs.

Computation first

research/tools/bmd_cube_local_smith.cpp computes the elementary divisors of the constraint matrix \(A\), and of \(A\) without its last column, over \(\mathbb F_p[[s]]\). It takes \(y_1=s\) or \(y_1=y_2+s\), with \(y_2,y_3\) random in \(\mathbb F_{1000003}\), and uses a local Smith reduction. Along both \(y_1=0\) and \(y_1=y_2\), for \(d\le7\) and \(d\le6\) (research/results/bmd-r34-collision-exponent/smith-n3.txt, smith-n3-diag.txt), the nonzero divisors are exactly

\[ 1,1,2,2,\ldots,d-1,d-1,d \qquad(\text{sum }d^2), \]

and they are the same without the last column. This suggests which lifts to look for. The kernel does not attribute divisors to rows, but the natural interpretation is that the divisor \(d-e\) belongs to the row \(z_1P\), where \(e=2Q+|r'|\) is the weight of \(P=T^Qz^{r'}\).

The collision lifts

Fix \(n\ge2\) and \(d\ge1\), work in characteristic \(\ne2\), and let \(F'=F(y_2,\ldots,y_n)\) and \(O=F'[[s]]\) with \(y_1=s\). Let \(V=V_{n,d}(O)\) be the free \(O\)-module with basis \(T^Qz^r\) (\(2Q+|r|\le d\)), and \(\tau:V\to O[[T]]/(T^{N+1})\) the truncation, \(N=N_{2,n}(d)\). Then \(V=V_{n-1,d}\oplus z_1V_{n-1,d-1}\), with the first summand in the variables \(z_2,\ldots,z_n\). By the \(n=1\) case of the law (below), for each \(k\ge1\) there is a unique \(\Psi_k=A_k(U)+(1+B_k(U))\,r_0(U)\) with \(A_k,B_k\in UF[U]\), \(\deg A_k\le\lfloor k/2\rfloor\), \(\deg B_k\le\lfloor(k-1)/2\rfloor\), and \(\Psi_k=\varepsilon_kU^k+O(U^{k+1})\), \(\varepsilon_k\ne0\).

Working lemma (lem:cube-collision-lifts). For each basis element \(P=T^Qz^{r'}\) of \(V_{n-1,d-1}\), of weight \(e=2Q+|r'|\), the element \(L_P=P\cdot\Psi_{d-e}(sT)\) lies in \(V\). It is congruent to \(z_1P\) modulo \(sV\), and \(\tau(L_P)\equiv0\pmod{s^{d-e}}\). Consequently:

  1. If the constraint matrix has rank \(N\) (otherwise \(g=0\) and the bound is vacuous), the elementary divisors of \(\tau\) over \(O\) have sum at least \(E_n(d)=\sum_P(d-e(P))\), so \(v_{y_1}(g)\ge E_n(d)\) for the gcd \(g\) of the maximal minors.
  2. For \(n=3\), equality holds, with divisors exactly \(\{d-e(P)\}\) and zeros, so \(v_{y_1}(g)=E_3(d)=d^2\).

Proof.

  • The \(n=1\) case. The polynomials of degree \(\le k\) in \(r_0(U)\) (one variable, \(y=1\)) have basis \(U^b\) (\(2b\le k\)) and \(U^br_0\) (\(2b+1\le k\)), \(k+1\) elements. On the genus-0 curve \(w^2=1+4U\) they form \(L(k\infty)\), since \(\infty\) is a single place at which \(w\) has a simple pole. An element of order \(\ge k+1\) at \(p_0\) would lie in \(L(k\infty-(k+1)p_0)=0\). So the orders are \(0,\ldots,k\), and the element \(\Psi\) of order exactly \(k\) is unique up to scalar.
    • Its \(r_0\)-coefficient at \(U^0\) is nonzero. Otherwise \(\Psi=U\Psi'\) with \(\Psi'\) of degree \(\le k-2\) and order \(k-1\), which is impossible. (For \(k=1\), \(\Psi=r_0\).)
    • Normalizing that coefficient to 1 gives \(A_k,B_k\). Order \(\ge1\) forces \(A_k(0)=0\).
  • The lifts. With \(U=sT\):
    \[ L_P=\sum_b\alpha_bs^b\,T^bP+\sum_b\beta_bs^b\,T^bz_1P,\qquad \beta_0=1,\ \alpha_0=0. \]
    The terms are basis elements of \(V\) of weights \(e+2b\le d\) and \(e+2b+1\le d\), by the degree bounds with \(k=d-e\). So \(L_P\in V\) and \(L_P\equiv z_1P\pmod{sV}\). Also \(\Psi_{d-e}(sT)\) is divisible by \((sT)^{d-e}\), and \(P\) does not involve \(s\), so \(\tau(L_P)\equiv0\pmod{s^{d-e}}\).
  • Part 1. The set \(\{\)basis of \(V_{n-1,d}\}\cup\{L_P\}\) is an \(O\)-basis of \(V\): it is unitriangular with respect to \(\{\)basis of \(V_{n-1,d}\}\cup\{z_1P\}\). Let \(V^+\supseteq V\) be spanned by the basis of \(V_{n-1,d}\) and the \(s^{-(d-e(P))}L_P\). Then \(\tau(V^+)\subseteq O^{N+1}\), and \(\mathrm{length}(V^+/V)=E_n(d)\). With \(\tau\) injective over \(\mathrm{Frac}(O)\) (rank \(N\)), \(\tau(V^+)/\tau(V)\cong V^+/V\). The sum of the elementary divisors is the length of the saturation of \(\tau(V)\) modulo \(\tau(V)\), which is at least this length. It equals \(v_{y_1}(g)\), computed in the local ring at \(y_1=0\), whose completion is \(O\).
  • Part 2 (\(n=3\)). The lattice \(\tau(V^+)\) is saturated if and only if the images modulo \(s\) of its basis are \(F'\)-independent in \(F'^{\,N+1}\). Reduce these images modulo \(s\).
    • The basis of \(V_{2,d}\) reduces to itself, the functions \(T^Qz^{r'}\) of weight \(\le d\).
    • The series \(s^{-(d-e)}L_P=P\cdot s^{-(d-e)}\Psi_{d-e}(sT)\in O[[T]]\) has \(T^{j}\)-coefficient \(\psi_js^{j-(d-e)}\) times \(P\), where \(\Psi_{d-e}=\sum_j\psi_jU^j\) with \(\psi_j=0\) for \(j<d-e\). So it reduces modulo \(s\) to \(\varepsilon_{d-e}T^{d-e}P=\varepsilon_{d-e}T^{d-Q-|r'|}z^{r'}\), a basis element of weight \(2d-e\in[d+1,2d]\).
    • Distinct \(P\) give distinct elements, since for fixed \(r'\) the map \(Q\mapsto d-Q-|r'|\) is injective, and none has weight \(\le d\).
    • So the reduction consists of \(N=4d\) distinct basis elements of \(V_{2,2d}\) over \(F'\). By the \(n=2\) law (square theorem and degree one), the orders of \(V_{2,2d}\) are \(0,\ldots,4d\). So truncation modulo \(T^{4d+1}\) is injective on \(V_{2,2d}\), and in particular on the reduction.
    • Hence \(\tau(V^+)\) is saturated in \(O^{N+1}\). The saturation of \(\tau(V)\) is therefore \(\tau(V^+)\), and the elementary divisors are the \(d-e(P)\).
    • For \(n=3\), \(V_{2,d-1}\) has one basis element of weight 0 and two of each weight \(1,\ldots,d-1\), so \(E_3(d)=d+2\sum_{e=1}^{d-1}(d-e)=d^2\). \(\square\)

The theorem

Theorem (thm:cube-dimension-three-boundary-threshold). Over every field of characteristic \(\ne2\) and for every \(d\ge1\), the gcd of the boundary minors is \(g_{3,d}=c\,(y_1y_2y_3)^{d^2}\prod_{i<j}(y_i-y_j)^{d^2}\) with \(c\ne0\) a constant. The primitive boundary solution has \(W_N=\Delta_{3,d}/g_{3,d}\) of degree \(d^2-1\). So on \(\{0,1\}^3\), at \(m=4d\), the savings are \(\ge d+1\) exactly when \(\ell\ge d^2-1\).

Proof.

  • The divisors \(y_i=0\). \(v_{y_1}(g)=d^2\) by the lemma, and the same holds for \(y_2\) and \(y_3\) by permuting the variables.
  • The divisors \(y_i=y_j\). Take \(y_1=y_2\), with \(a=4y\). Substitute \(T=\tilde T/(1-a_2\tilde T)\), a change of parameter fixing \(0\). Then \(1+a_iT=(1+(a_i-a_2)\tilde T)/(1-a_2\tilde T)\).
    • With \(\tilde w_i=(1+\tilde a_i\tilde T)^{1/2}\) and \(\tilde a=(a_1-a_2,\,-a_2,\,a_3-a_2)\), this gives \(w_1=\tilde w_1/\tilde w_2\), \(w_2=1/\tilde w_2\) and \(w_3=\tilde w_3/\tilde w_2\).
    • Multiplication by \(\tilde w_2^{\,d}\) sends \(T^Qw^r\) to \(\tilde T^Q\tilde w_1^{r_1}\tilde w_3^{r_3}\tilde w_2^{\,d-2Q-|r|}\). Reducing \(\tilde w_2^2=1-a_2\tilde T\), this is \(\tilde T^Q\tilde w^{(r_1,\epsilon,r_3)}\) (with \(\epsilon\equiv d-|r|\bmod 2\)) plus basis elements with larger \(\tilde T\)-power. Hence \(f\mapsto\tilde w_2^{\,d}f\) maps \(V_d(a)\) onto \(\tilde V_d(\tilde a)\) by a unitriangular change of basis.
    • The substitution and the multiplication by the unit series \(\tilde w_2^{\,d}\) act on truncated series by unitriangular matrices over \(\mathbb Z[\tfrac12][a_2]\). The changes between \(z\) and \(w\) bases are triangular with powers of 2 on the diagonal.
    • So \(A(a)=G\,\tilde A(\tilde a)\,H\), where \(\det G\) is \(\pm\) a power of 2 and \(H\) is invertible over \(\mathbb Z[\tfrac12][a]\). By Cauchy–Binet the ideal of maximal minors is unchanged, so \(g(a)=u\cdot\tilde g(\tilde a)\) with \(u\) a unit.
    • The divisor \(a_1=a_2\) is \(\tilde a_1=0\). Apply the lemma over \(F'=F(\tilde a_2,\tilde a_3)\) with \(\tilde y=\tilde a/4\); the factor 4 does not change the valuation. The \(n=2\) law is used for the independent pair \((-a_2,a_3-a_2)\). So \(v_{y_1-y_2}(g)=d^2\), and the other diagonals follow by symmetry.
  • Conclusion. By the proposition, \(g\) has no other prime factors, and \(g\) is homogeneous, so \(g=c\prod D^{d^2}\) with \(\deg g=6d^2\). Every entry \((Q,r;c)\) of \(A\) is homogeneous of degree \(c-Q\), so the nonzero \(\Delta_{3,d}\) has degree \(\sum_{c<4d}c-\sum Q\). Here \(\sum_{c<4d}c=8d^2-2d\). Also \(\sum Q=(d-1)^2\) for both parities of \(d\): there are 8 such \(r\) when \(d-2Q\ge3\), 7 when \(d-2Q=2\), 4 when \(d-2Q=1\) and 1 when \(d-2Q=0\). So \(\deg\Delta_{3,d}=7d^2-1\). The threshold-ideal lemma (Part 2, valid since \(\mathrm{Ord}_3(d)\) is an initial segment in characteristic \(\ne2\)) gives \(\ell_0=7d^2-1-6d^2=d^2-1\). \(\square\)

This proves part 1 of conj:cube-dimension-three-thresholds. It matches the kernel thresholds 3, 8, 15, 24 and the line computations through \(d=10\).

Every dimension. Part 1 of the lemma holds for every \(n\), when the constraint matrix has rank \(N\). The Möbius argument goes through verbatim for the divisor \(y_1=y_j\): substitute \(T=\tilde T/(1-a_j\tilde T)\), multiply by \(\tilde w_j^{\,d}\), and let \(\epsilon\) take the place of \(r_j\). So \(v_D(g_{n,d})\ge E_n(d)\) for each of the \(\binom{n+1}2\) collision divisors. Where \(\mathrm{Ord}_n(d)\) is an initial segment, the boundary threshold is then at most \(\deg\Delta_{n,d}-\binom{n+1}2E_n(d)\). For \(n=4\), \(E_4(d)=1,5,13,25,41=d^2+(d-1)^2\) for \(d=1,\ldots,5\), exactly the exponents found on the test lines in the previous entry. The line gcds certify \(\deg g\le10E_4(d)\) over \(\mathbb F_{1000003}\). With the proved lower bound this gives \(\deg g=10E_4(d)\), and hence the thresholds \(0,15,55,115,195\), rigorously over \(\mathbb F_{1000003}\) for \(d\le5\) only. For \(n\ge4\) the \(n=2\) injectivity argument has no analogue, since \(\dim V_{3,2d}=8d\) exceeds \(N+1\). The equality of the line exponents with \(E_4(d)\) suggests that \(\tau(V^+)\) is still saturated. The proposition's genus-1 argument is also missing for \(n\ge4\).

General statement. thm:cube-dimension-three-boundary-threshold, for every \(d\ge1\) in characteristic \(\ne2\). lem:cube-collision-lifts, with the Möbius transport, gives the bound along every collision divisor for all \(n\) and \(d\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the threshold-ideal lemma (Part 2), the proposition on collision factors, the Hermite–Padé form, and the \(n=2\) law (square theorem and degree-one lemma). Claim searches (collision, elementary divisor, boundary threshold, saturation) found only this thread's results. The local Smith computation was done before the proof and fixed the pattern. A fresh-context referee checked every step and found none that breaks the theorem: the genus-0 case, the normalization of \(\Psi_k\), the weights of the lifts, the basis claim, the reduction modulo \(s\) and the saturation, the Möbius transport including its index bijection, and the degree count. It independently recomputed \(E_3\), \(E_4\), \(\sum Q\) and the \(n=4\) thresholds, and matched the data files. Its corrections are made: the rank hypothesis in Part 1; the reduction applied to series and the saturation criterion; the count of the \(r\); the weight attribution; the field and Cauchy–Binet in the diagonal case; the general-\(n\) transport; the scope of the \(n=4\) statement; the wording on saturation for \(n\ge4\); the general statement; the scheme-theoretic caveat on the division locus; and provenance headers for the data files.

Assessment. In dimension 3 the pre-stable threshold at each \(\kappa\)-boundary is now proved. The generator \(W_N=\Delta_{3,d}/g\) has degree \(d^2-1\) and vanishes, set-theoretically, exactly on the \(d\)-division locus of the point \(2c_1\), so the threshold is governed by the same elliptic curve as the stable law. Its identification with the division locus as a scheme, with multiplicities, is not proved. Order zero is the other end of the same family (\(\delta(3,k,0)=2k-\lfloor k/5\rfloor\), still conjectural), and the thresholds strictly between these two ends are open. The proof pattern suggests the next step. Solutions away from the boundary have larger rank, and their thresholds should come from the same saturation computation applied to several maximal minors.

25 September 2026 — Order zero on the 3-cube: a dual polynomial form, and δ(3,k,0) ≤ 2k − ⌊k/5⌋ by Vieta

Working proof of the order-zero upper bound for n = 3 in characteristic ≠ 2; the matching lower bound stays conjectural. Produced by Claude Code (Claude Opus 5.5, default reasoning).

Question. Part 2 of conj:cube-dimension-three-thresholds says that \(\delta(3,k,0)=2k-\lfloor k/5\rfloor\) for \(k\ge2\), in characteristic \(\ne2\). This is the other end of the threshold family, whose boundary end is proved. Which half can be proved, and by what mechanism? The test ends with a construction or with a reason none exists.

The dual polynomial form

Working lemma (lem:cube-dual-polynomial-form). On \(\{0,1\}^n\) over any field, let \(m=k-\ell-1\) and \(u=(u_1,\ldots,u_n)\), and put \(z^a(u)=\prod_{a_i=1}r_0(u_i)\) with \(r_0(u)=\sum_{j\ge1}(-1)^jC_{j-1}u^j\). The savings satisfy \(h(k,\ell)\ge h\) if and only if some polynomial \(\Phi=\sum_{j=\ell}^{\ell+m}\Phi_j\) (with \(\Phi_j\) homogeneous of degree \(j\), and \(\Phi_\ell\ne0\)) satisfies

\[ \bigl[\Phi\cdot z^a(u)\bigr]_{\ell+m-t}=0\qquad\text{for all }a\in\{0,1\}^n,\ t\ge0,\ |a|+2t\le h-1, \]

where \([\,\cdot\,]_j\) is the homogeneous component of degree \(j\).

Proof. By the threshold-ideal lemma, savings \(\ge h\) at \((k,\ell)\) holds exactly when there are homogeneous \(W_c\) of degree \(\ell+m-c\) (\(c=0,\ldots,m\)) with \(W_m\ne0\) and \(\sum_cW_cX_{a,c-t}=0\). Here \(X_{a,r}=[z^a]_r\). Put \(\Phi_{\ell+m-c}=W_c\). Then \(\sum_cW_cX_{a,c-t}=\sum_c[\Phi]_{\ell+m-c}[z^a]_{c-t}=[\Phi z^a]_{\ell+m-t}\), because the homogeneous parts of a product convolve: \([\Phi z^a]_{\ell+m-t}=\sum_j\Phi_j[z^a]_{\ell+m-t-j}\), and \(j=\ell+m-c\) runs over \(c=0,\ldots,m\). \(\square\)

At \(\ell=0\) this asks for \(\Phi\) with \(\Phi(0)=1\) and \(\deg\Phi\le m\). The conditions with \(a=0\) remove the top degrees. After dividing by \(u_i\), those with \(|a|=1\) and shift 0 say that one polynomial in an auxiliary variable \(s\), with coefficients in \(F[u]\), takes the same value at \(s=u_1,\ldots,u_n\). That is what Vieta's formulas produce.

The upper bound

Theorem (thm:cube-order-zero-upper-bound). Over every field of characteristic \(\ne2\) and for every \(k\ge2\), \(\delta(3,k,0)\le2k-\lfloor k/5\rfloor\).

Call \(P\) admissible for \(k\) if \(P(0)\ne0\) and \(P\) has (Hasse) multiplicity \(\ge k\) at every nonzero point of \(\{0,1\}^3\), so that \(\delta(3,k,0)\) is the least degree of such a \(P\).

Proof. Products of admissible polynomials are admissible. Multiplicities add at each point, because the lowest nonzero homogeneous part of the Taylor expansion of \(fg\) at a point is the product of those of \(f\) and \(g\). Degrees add, and the value at the origin stays nonzero. Put \(f(k)=2k-\lfloor k/5\rfloor\). Then \(f(5q+r)=9q+f(r)\), and every \(k\ge2\) is \(5q+r\) with \(q\ge0\) and \(r\in\{2,\ldots,6\}\). So it suffices to have admissible polynomials for \(k=5\) of degree \(\le9\) and for \(r=2,\ldots,6\) of degree \(\le f(r)=4,6,8,9,11\).

  • \(k=2\), degree 4. \((x_1-1)(x_2-1)(x_3-1)(x_1+x_2+x_3-1)\). Each point of weight 1 lies on two of the planes, points of weight 2 on two, and \((1,1,1)\) on three. The value at the origin is 1.
  • \(k=3\), degree 6. \(\prod_i(x_i-1)\prod_{i<j}(x_i+x_j-1)\). Points of weight 1 lie on \(1+2\) planes, weight 2 on \(2+2\), and \((1,1,1)\) on \(3+0\). The value at the origin is 1.
  • \(k=4\), degree 8. The square of the \(k=2\) polynomial.
  • \(k=5\), degree 9, savings 2 at \(m=4\). By the lemma with \(h=2\), \(\ell=0\), \(m=4\), a suitable \(\Phi\) is needed. The conditions are \([\Phi]_4=0\) and \([\Phi\,r_0(u_i)]_4=0\) for \(i=1,2,3\). Take \(\Phi=1+\tfrac52e_1+5e_2+5e_3\), with \(e_j\) the elementary symmetric polynomials of \(u\). Since \(r_0(u)=-u+u^2-2u^3+5u^4-\cdots\),
    \[ [\Phi\,r_0(u_i)]_4=-u_i\Phi_3+u_i^2\Phi_2-2u_i^3\Phi_1+5u_i^4=5u_i\,(u_i^3-e_1u_i^2+e_2u_i-e_3)=5u_i\prod_j(u_i-u_j)=0. \]
    So savings \(\ge2\) at \((k,\ell)=(5,0)\), that is, \(\delta\le3+8-2=9\).
  • \(k=6\), degree 11, savings 2 at \(m=5\). Take \(\Phi=1+7(e_2-e_1^2)+14(e_3-e_1e_2)-14e_1e_3\). Then \([\Phi]_5=0\), and \([\Phi\,r_0(u_i)]_5\) equals
    \[ -u_i\Phi_4+u_i^2\Phi_3-2u_i^3\Phi_2-14u_i^5=-14u_i\,(u_i+e_1)\prod_j(u_i-u_j)=0. \]
    So \(\delta(3,6,0)\le3+10-2=11\). \(\square\)

The \(k=5\) construction divides by 2, and \(k=6\) needs no division. In characteristic 5 the \(k=5\) polynomial is \(\Phi=1\), which works because \(C_3=5\) vanishes there.

What is and is not shown

  • The upper bound is proved. Here \(\delta(3,k,0)\approx9k/5\). The \(\mathbb F_2\) value is \(3+\sum_{j<3}\lfloor(k-1)/2^j\rfloor\approx7k/4\). Over \(\mathbb F_2\) the bound fails at \(k=5\) (10 against 9); the referee's check for \(k<200\) found no other failure. The \(\mathbb F_2\) value lies below \(f(k)\) at \(k=4\) and from \(k=8\) on. So it is the conjectured equality, not the bound alone, that is specific to characteristic \(\ne2\).
  • The lower bound is only computed. The kernel runs of the earlier entry give \(\delta(3,k,0)=f(k)\) exactly for \(2\le k\le30\) over \(\mathbb F_{101}\) and \(k\le32\) over \(\mathbb F_3\). So the upper bound is attained there.
  • The case \(h=2\). The same Vieta argument shows that in characteristic \(\ne2\) no \(\Phi\) exists for \(h=2\), \(m=3\).
    • The condition \([\Phi]_3=0\) forces \(\Phi_3=0\).
    • The conditions \([\Phi\,r_0(u_i)]_3=0\) then say \(-2s^2+\Phi_1s=\Phi_2\) at the three distinct points \(s=u_1,u_2,u_3\) of \(F(u)\).
    • A quadratic in \(s\) with leading coefficient \(-2\ne0\) cannot take one value at three distinct points.
    In characteristic 2, \(\Phi=1\) works (\(\delta(3,4,0)=7\) over \(\mathbb F_2\)), so the hypothesis matters. This is the lower bound at \(h=2\), which \(\kappa_3(3)=1\) already gives.
  • General \(h\). The lower bound for general \(h\) needs the conditions with \(|a|\ge2\) and \(t\ge1\) as well.

General statement. thm:cube-order-zero-upper-bound, for every \(k\ge2\) in characteristic \(\ne2\). lem:cube-dual-polynomial-form holds for all \(n\), \(k\) and \(\ell\).

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the threshold-ideal lemma (Part 1, the condition system), of which the dual form is a reindexing and so is recorded as dependent on it, and the \(\mathbb F_2\) order-zero value. Claim searches (order zero, Sauermann–Wigderson, hyperplane cover) found the \(\mathbb F_2\) results only. One web search found Clifton and Huang on hyperplane covers of the cube minus the origin; its abstract gives no exact \(n=3\) formula, so it is not used. A fresh-context referee checked the lemma's bookkeeping, the three plane products by hand, both \(\Phi\) identities with sympy, the reduction and the data (no mismatch for \(k\le30\) over \(\mathbb F_{101}\) and \(k\le32\) over \(\mathbb F_3\)), and found nothing that breaks a result. Its corrections are made: the \(\mathbb F_2\) comparison; the \(h=2\) exclusion, with its use of characteristic \(\ne2\); the name of the auxiliary variable; the scope of the equal-value remark; the definition of admissible; and the assessment's wording.

Assessment. The dual form turns the per-order problem into symmetric-function identities, and at order zero Vieta's formulas give the two \(h=2\) base constructions. The natural next step is the order-zero lower bound: show that no \(\Phi\) with \(\Phi(0)=1\) and \(\deg\Phi\le5h-7\) meets the conditions for \(h\). Plausibly this uses a symmetric reduction together with a count of the equal-value conditions. The condition set is invariant when \(a\) and \(u\) are permuted together, so averaging over \(S_3\) gives a symmetric solution in characteristic 0 or \(>3\).
